## ENERGY MARKETS

The energy landscape is rapidly evolving, driven by technological advancements, regulatory reforms, and changing consumer preferences. In this section, we explore the dynamics of energy markets, from traditional trading models to emerging trends in open energy markets.

From market-based trading to virtual power plant strategies, each business model reflects unique approaches to energy procurement and utilization. We analyze the role of merchant trading, arbitrage trading, and day-ahead markets in shaping market efficiency and resilience.

Moreover, we delve into the paradigm shift brought about by renewable energy markets and virtual power plant trading, highlighting the pivotal role of renewable sources in decentralizing energy production.

Central to our discussion is the concept of open energy markets, characterized by deregulation and competition. We examine how these markets facilitate peer-to-peer energy trading and consumer empowerment, directing the positive change toward a more efficient and sustainable ecosystem for energy.

### Market-Based Trading

Market-based trading is how energy has been traded for most of the twentieth century. It involves buying and selling energy commodities in various wholesale markets. Traditionally, this market consisted of two main actors:

1.  The energy distributor or retailer who is able to deliver energy commodities
    
2.  The end consumers who buy the retailed energy and use it
    

A diagram of the flow of energy in market-based trading is shown in [Figure 3.1](#chapter3.xhtml#f3_1).

![FIGURE 3.1 The flow of energy in market-based trading.](images/fig3_1.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.1](#chapter3.xhtml#b3fig3_1) The flow of energy in market-based trading.

### Merchant Trading

Merchant energy traders are energy traders with energy mining, production, or generation assets, who participate in the market directly. Merchant traders optimize the operation of these assets by buying and selling energy in the wholesale market and entering into long-term contracts with counterparties. They use their physical assets to generate revenue by selling energy directly into the market, rather than solely relying on trading contracts \[[15](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_15)\]. [Figure 3.2](#chapter3.xhtml#f3_2) illustrates the direct relation between energy producers and the retail markets.

![FIGURE 3.2 The flow of energy in merchant trading.](images/fig3_2.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.2](#chapter3.xhtml#b3fig3_2) The flow of energy in merchant trading.

Reflecting on the historical practices of merchant energy trading unveils a nuanced narrative of market dynamics and regulatory challenges. In examining the strategies employed by merchant traders, it becomes evident that their formidable control over energy assets often translated into opportunities for exploitation \[[16](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_16)\]. Through strategic maneuvers such as market manipulation and regulatory arbitrage, merchant traders wielded considerable influence, occasionally at the expense of end consumers and the integrity of the retail market. The historical record reveals instances where these actors prioritized profit maximization over market stability, resulting in adverse outcomes such as price volatility and supply disruptions. Indeed, the historical landscape of merchant energy trading underscores the importance of vigilant oversight and regulatory frameworks to curb potential abuses and safeguard the interests of consumers and market participants alike.

### Arbitrage Trading

Arbitrage trading exploits the price variations between different markets, locations, or time periods. For example, a trader might buy energy in a low-price market and sell it in a higher-priced market to profit from the price differential \[[17](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_17)\]. The seller might not necessarily be a part of the energy industry. This trading technique is the product of high variance in supply and demand for energy commodities in different parts of the world. This simple exploit can be present in both real-time and day-ahead markets. [Figure 3.3](#chapter3.xhtml#f3_3) visually simplifies this concept.

![FIGURE 3.3 Arbitrage trading.](images/fig3_3.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.3](#chapter3.xhtml#b3fig3_3) Arbitrage trading.

Arbitrage energy trading has played a pivotal role in fostering a more globalized energy market. By capitalizing on price differentials between regions or markets, this trading technique facilitates the efficient allocation of resources and encourages the flow of energy across borders \[[18](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_18)\]. As energy can be bought in regions with lower prices and sold where prices are higher, arbitrage trading helps equalize prices across different locations. This process incentivizes market participants to seek out profitable opportunities, thereby promoting market liquidity and price convergence. Ultimately, arbitrage energy trading contributes to the harmonization of energy prices worldwide and the creation of a more interconnected and integrated energy market landscape.

### Day-Ahead Market

There is one property in the energy market that, if changed, changes the whole paradigm of the market. In this market model, the agreement for a trade is set one day prior to delivery. This market emerged when the energy industry became large enough for energy prices to be extremely sensitive to change. Only trading one day in advance and not having any long-term contracts allows traders to forecast the gains and market state a lot more easily \[[19](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_19)\]. This kind of market is also especially advantageous for renewable energy producers, as it enables them to adapt their generation schedules in response to changing weather patterns and energy demand forecasts. For example, if weather forecasts predict a surge in solar generation the following day, solar power producers can adjust their output commitments accordingly in the day-ahead market, maximizing the utilization of renewable resources. Similarly, grid operators can procure additional backup power or adjust demand-side management strategies based on anticipated renewable energy availability, enhancing grid stability and reliability.

In the day-ahead market of electricity trading, market players participate in the purchase and sale of electricity for delivery on the subsequent day. This market plays a vital role in guaranteeing a dependable and effective electricity provision. Participants, such as generators, utilities, and traders, provide bids and offers that state the amount of power they are willing to purchase or sell, along with the corresponding price. These bids and offers are usually made for various time intervals throughout the day. The bids and offers are influenced by factors like expected demand patterns, the availability of generation resources, fuel costs, and limitations within the gearbox system. The market operator subsequently gathers and analyses these bids and offers to ascertain the most efficient distribution of resources. The objective is to align the electrical supply with the projected demand while minimizing the total expenses. The operator uses advanced algorithms and market-clearing procedures to determine the clearing price for each time interval by analyzing the point where the supply and demand curves intersect. The clearing price is the specific price at which all accepted bids and offers are met, guaranteeing that the amount of electricity provided is equal to the amount required. After the completion of the clearing process, market participants are notified about the acceptance of their bids and offers, along with the corresponding clearing prices. This knowledge enables them to strategically organize their generation and consuming activities for the subsequent day \[[20](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_20)\]. In addition, the DAM results offer significant insights into market dynamics, price patterns, and system conditions, empowering participants to make well-informed decisions in other energy markets and operations. The day-ahead market is essential for ensuring grid reliability, maximizing resource utilization, and promoting competition in the energy industry.

### Renewable Energy Market

This model involves buying and selling renewable energy attributes such as renewable energy certificates (RECs) or guarantees of origin (GOs) \[[21](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_21)\]. Renewable energy traders facilitate the transfer of these attributes between renewable energy generators and buyers looking to meet sustainability goals or regulatory requirements. Using renewable sources introduced decentralization and distributed generation in the energy market. The availability of renewable resources means that most places around the world are able to generate energy. Moreover, renewable energy production occurs closer to the point of consumption. Consequently, many countries have implemented supportive policies, incentives, and regulatory frameworks to stimulate investment in renewable energy projects, such as feed-in tariffs, renewable portfolio standards, tax incentives, and carbon pricing mechanisms. Collectively, these changes empower individuals, communities, and businesses to become energy producers (prosumersAn individual who consumes and produces value). This shift is what led to many energy markets going open.

### Virtual Power Plant (VPPVirtual power plant) Trading

Virtual power plant (VPPVirtual power plant) trading involves aggregating distributed energy resources and demand response assets. Traders optimize the use of these resources by participating in energy markets, providing grid services, or entering into contracts with utilities and grid operators \[[22](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_22)\]. Within the realm of renewable energy trading, VPPsVirtual power plant enable the seamless integration of intermittent renewable generation into energy markets by aggregating surplus energy from distributed renewable sources. By intelligently managing these resources, VPPVirtual power plant operators can participate in energy markets, offering renewable energy generation capacity, ancillary services, and grid-balancing support to utilities and grid operators.

This form of trading has played a crucial role in globalizing the electricity market by combining distributed energy resources (DERs) and optimizing their usage. These VPPsVirtual power plant actively engage in energy markets by providing excess renewable energy and additional services for utilities and grid operators. This active engagement enables the transmission of electricity across national boundaries and fosters increased integration and collaboration across regional electricity markets \[[23](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_23)\]. Additionally, virtual power plants have a crucial function in maintaining grid equilibrium and facilitating the incorporation of renewable energy sources by effectively regulating the fluctuations in renewable generation and offering stability services to the system. VPPVirtual power plant operators can use sophisticated forecasting and optimization algorithms to detect possibilities for trading electricity across borders. This can help in decentralizing and strengthening the electricity system.

### Open Energy Markets

Deregulated or liberalized energy markets are those where competition is allowed to determine prices, and multiple participants—including generators, retailers, and consumers—can freely buy and sell energy commodities. These markets aim to enhance efficiency, foster innovation, and provide consumers with more choices and potentially lower prices by introducing competition into the energy sector. Open energy markets contrast with regulated markets where prices and/or operations are controlled or influenced by government authorities or regulated monopolies. Open energy markets provide opportunities for arbitrage trading and include a day-ahead market \[[24](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_24)\].

Peer-to-peer (P2P) energy trading thrives within the regulatory and technological framework of open energy markets. These markets, characterized by supportive regulatory environments accommodating diverse market participants, facilitate direct interactions among consumers for energy exchange. Regulatory provisions ensuring grid accessibility, along with standards for metering and billing, create an enabling environment for peer-to-peer transactions \[[25](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_25)\]. Digital platforms and marketplaces within open energy markets serve as pivotal infrastructures, streamlining peer-to-peer engagements by facilitating matching, negotiation, and transaction management. Technological advancements, particularly in smart grid technologies and blockchain-based solutions, augment the feasibility and security of peer-to-peer energy trading. Furthermore, consumer empowerment in open energy markets, driven by greater choice and control over energy decisions, fuels the emergence of peer-to-peer energy trading, which allows individuals to transition from ordinary energy consumers to prosumers engaged in decentralized energy production and exchange. Consequently, peer-to-peer energy trading capitalizes on the openness, technological innovation, and consumer-centricity inherent in open energy markets, fostering efficiency, resilience, and sustainability within the energy ecosystem.

### Peer-to-Peer Energy Trading

Peer-to-peer energy trading revolutionizes the traditional energy distribution model by enabling direct transactions between energy producers and consumers within a community. In this decentralized approach, households equipped with renewable energy sources, such as solar panels, can trade excess electricity with their neighbors, fostering sustainability and resilience in the energy ecosystem.

An example of peer-to-peer energy trading can be found in a district that aims toward a greener grid where each house generates most of its own electricity using a photovoltaic (PVPhotovoltaic) panel that can supply green energy for its average demand. Each house still relies on the national grid, which uses fossil resources to meet the rest of the demand and ensure higher availability rates. The problem is that on many occasions, the load drops and suddenly the energy generated by the solar panels is going to waste at one home while at another home, they’re using all their generated energy next to using energy from the national grid to cover their demand. For a district that aims to be green, there is a heavy dependence on the national grid to supply energy. Now, there can be solutions to this problem. For example, each house can have more powerful PVPhotovoltaic panels, but it would be extremely expensive to have a panel that would only be fully utilized once every now and then. Another solution would be to rid all the homes from the solar panels and create a renewable power plant that is powerful enough to power the entire district, but no house would want to give up its freely generated electricity. The proper solution tackles minimizing wasted energy that is generated during load drops. There should be a way to maximize the usage of electricity generated from all homes while creating benefits for the residents. This is where the concept of energy trading comes in. All houses, acting as prosumers, would be connected to the same grid so that the surplus produced by one prosumer can fulfill another prosumer's demand. This opens the window for each prosumer to monetize the surplus they generate and for deficientNot having enough of the demanded material or quality; someone with deficit prosumers to fulfill their demands by buying cheaper, and greener, energy. [Figure 3.4](#chapter3.xhtml#f3_4) shows how prosumers connected on the same grid can be able to supply energy for each other.

![FIGURE 3.4 Individuals connected on a grid with sources of generating renewable energy.](images/fig3_4.jpg)

[FIGURE 3.4](#chapter3.xhtml#b3fig3_4) Individuals connected on a grid with sources of generating renewable energy.

Peer-to-peer trading offers an avenue to monetize surplus production, turning them into active participants in the energy market. This not only provides financial incentives but also promotes a sense of ownership and empowerment. Moreover, by facilitating the exchange of renewable energy among households, peer-to-peer trading maximizes the usage of renewable resources, reducing dependency on fossil fuels and contributing to a cleaner, greener energy mix.

An example of existing peer-to-peer trading systems is Piclo Flex, owned by Piclo. It is an independent marketplace for flexible energy services. System operators (such as National Grid ESO \[[26](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_26)\], UKPN \[[27](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_27)\], and a growing number internationally) are able to conveniently source energy from other service providers during deficiencies. Their market is auction-based where the energy is sold to the highest bidder regardless of what the optimum transaction to balance the grid is. The system proposed in this document aims to optimally balance the grid to match demand with minimum generated waste while creating benefit for prosumers \[[28](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_28)\].

## FUTURE APPLICATIONS

Energy trading platforms hold immense potential to revolutionize the renewable energy landscape. By facilitating peer-to-peer exchange of surplus renewable energy within local communities, these platforms promote efficient distribution and utilization. This section explores how energy trading aligns with UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and delves into its future applications, considerations, and potential risks.

### Alignment With UN SDGs

Energy trading serves as a pivotal instrument in advancing the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), addressing various societal, environmental, and economic challenges \[[29](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_29)\]. By facilitating the exchange of renewable energy resources within communities and across regions, energy trading platforms contribute significantly to the achievement of several SDGs, particularly those related to clean energy, sustainable urban development, climate action, and global partnerships.

*   **SDG 7: Accessible and Affordable Energy**
    
    SDG 7 strives to ensure that everyone can access energy that is both economical and environmentally friendly. It encourages the diversification of renewable energy sources and adopting of clean technologies to combat climate change while improving energy efficiency \[[30](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_30)\].
    
*   **SDG 11: Building Sustainable Urban Areas**
    
    SDG 11 aims to create cities and communities that are inclusive, safe, and environmentally sustainable. It stresses the importance of sustainable urban development, access to green spaces, efficient transportation, and the adoption of eco-friendly practices in construction and infrastructure \[[31](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_31)\].
    
*   **SDG 13: Addressing Climate Challenges**
    
    SDG 13 tackles the pressing need to fight climate change. It calls for actions to enhance resilience and adaptation to climate-related risks and disasters, alongside efforts to raise awareness, education, and capacity-building for climate change mitigation \[[32](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_32)\].
    
*   **SDG 17: Fostering Collaborative Partnerships**
    
    SDG 17 highlights the significance of collaborating together toward achieving sustainable development objectives. It stresses the importance of mobilizing resources, transferring technology, building capacities, and fostering cooperation among governments, civil society, and the private sector to address systemic issues and promote sustainability \[[33](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_33)\].
    

### Considerations

As energy trading evolves, careful consideration must be given to policy and regulatory frameworks, environmental and social impacts, and technological integration. Balancing market dynamics with regulatory requirements is essential for fostering innovation and ensuring equitable access to energy resources. Moreover, addressing environmental and social implications is crucial for promoting sustainable development and community empowerment.

#### Optimizing renewable energy utilization

The future of energy trading holds immense promise for revolutionizing the energy landscape toward sustainability and efficiency. By enabling direct peer-to-peer transactions, surplus energy of renewable origins can be efficiently distributed and utilized within local communities. This not only reduces wastage of renewable energy but also promotes self-sufficiency and resilience within microgrid networks. Advanced algorithms and smart contracts can facilitate real-time energy trading \[[34](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_34)\], dynamically matching supply with demand while ensuring grid stability and reliability.

#### Empowering energy prosumers

Energy trading platforms empower individuals and businesses to become active participants in the energy market as both consumers and producers, commonly referred to as prosumers. By leveraging renewable energy generation assets such as solar panels or wind turbines, prosumers can generate their electricity and engage in peer-to-peer trading to monetize their surplus energy \[[35](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_35)\]. This democratization of energy production and distribution fosters a more inclusive and sustainable energy ecosystem, reducing dependence on centralized utilities and promoting community-driven energy initiatives.

#### Grid balancing and demand response

Grid balancing and demand response mechanisms are crucial for managing renewable energy sources effectively. Unlike fossil fuels, renewable energy generation is often intermittent and less predictable \[[36](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_36)\]. Solar energy responds poorly to demand changes. Therefore, efficient grid balancing becomes crucial to match supply with fluctuating demand patterns, ensuring grid stability and reliability in renewable rich environments. By incentivizing flexible energy consumption behaviors and enabling real-time energy trading, these mechanisms facilitate the integration of renewable energy into the grid while minimizing wastage and optimizing utilization. Automated demand-side management strategies, coupled with intelligent pricing mechanisms, can encourage consumers to adjust their energy consumption patterns in response to renewable energy availability, thereby enhancing grid resilience and reducing reliance on conventional power sources.

#### Integration with emerging technologies

The future of energy trading is intertwined with emerging technologies such as blockchain, artificial intelligence, and the Internet of Things (IoT). Blockchain-based smart contracts provide a secure and transparent framework for executing peer-to-peer energy transactions, ensuring trust and immutability. Artificial intelligence-driven algorithms and statistical models can go through the vast amounts of data generated from the energy industry to optimize energy trading strategies, predict market trends, and automate decision-making processes. IoT-enabled devices, including smart meters and sensors, enable real-time monitoring and control of energy assets, facilitating seamless integration into energy trading networks.

#### Policy and regulatory considerations

As energy trading evolves, policymakers and regulators will need to adapt existing frameworks to accommodate new market dynamics and technological innovations. Clear and supportive regulatory frameworks are essential to foster innovation, ensure market integrity, and protect consumer interests \[[37](#chapter3.xhtml#r3_37)\]. Additionally, policies pushing for interoperability, standardization, and market transparency can facilitate the widespread adoption of energy trading platforms and encourage investment in renewable energy infrastructure.

#### Environmental and social impacts

Beyond economic considerations, energy trading has profound environmental and social implications. By promoting the move toward renewable energy, energy trading contributes to mitigating climate change and improving air quality. Moreover, community-based energy trading initiatives empower local communities, encourage social equity, and foster sustainable development. However, it is essential to address potential challenges such as energy poverty, digital divide, and environmental justice to ensure that the benefits of energy trading are equitably distributed across society.

This section delves into the future applications of energy trading, focusing particularly on its role in managing renewable resources. We explore how energy trading platforms are poised to revolutionize the renewable energy landscape, enabling efficient distribution and utilization of surplus energy within local communities. Through the lens of emerging technologies like blockchain and artificial intelligence, we uncover how these platforms empower individuals and businesses to actively participate in renewable energy production and distribution. Join us as we navigate the evolving landscape of energy trading, envisioning a future where renewable resources drive sustainability and resilience in our energy ecosystem.

### Risks

It's crucial to recognize that with the innovation in the field of energy trading, there are inherent risks. There are complexities and uncertainties that accompany the adoption and implementation of this concept. While energy trading holds promise in promoting renewable energy adoption, enhancing economic competitiveness, and fostering social inclusivity, it also presents a myriad of challenges that demand careful consideration and proactive mitigation strategies.

#### Market volatility

Energy markets experience constant changes influenced by a myriad of factors. These fluctuations stem from shifts in both supply and demand dynamics, geopolitical tensions, and alterations in regulatory frameworks. These fluctuations can lead to price volatility, liquidity issues, and counterparty risks for energy traders, potentially impacting their profitability and financial stability. Effective risk management strategies, such as diversification and hedging, are essential to mitigate these risks and ensure the long-term viability of energy trading activities.

#### Regulatory compliance

Energy trading activities are subject to complex and evolving regulatory frameworks, including market rules, environmental regulations, and data privacy laws. Compliance with these regulations is essential to ensure the legality, transparency, and fairness of energy trading operations and maintain the trust and confidence of stakeholders. However, navigating regulatory compliance requirements can be challenging, particularly in a rapidly changing regulatory landscape with overlapping jurisdictional boundaries. Therefore, energy traders must stay informed about regulatory developments and implement robust compliance programs to mitigate regulatory risks and uphold ethical standards.

#### Cybersecurity threats

Digitalized energy trading platforms are vulnerable to cybersecurity threats, including hacking, data breaches, and ransomware attacks. These threats can compromise the integrity, confidentiality, and availability of critical energy infrastructure, disrupting energy supply chains and causing significant financial and reputational damage. Robust cybersecurity measures, such as encryption, authentication, and intrusion detection systems, are necessary to protect energy trading platforms from cyber threats and safeguard sensitive information and operations.

## CONCLUSION

In conclusion, peer-to-peer energy trading represents a transformative approach to energy distribution, enabling direct transactions between producers and consumers within local communities. By leveraging renewable energy sources and facilitating surplus energy exchange, these platforms promote sustainability, resilience, and community empowerment in the energy ecosystem. Looking ahead, the future applications of energy trading hold immense promise for revolutionizing renewable resource management. Through alignment with the United Nations’ Sustainable Development Goals, energy trading platforms can contribute significantly to achieving global objectives related to accessible and affordable energy, sustainable urban development, climate action, and collaborative partnerships.

However, realizing the full potential of energy trading requires careful consideration of policy and regulatory frameworks, environmental and social impacts, technological integration, and risk management strategies. Balancing market dynamics with regulatory requirements, addressing environmental and social implications, and effectively managing risks are essential for fostering innovation and ensuring equitable access to clean energy resources.

From economic, environmental, and social standpoints, energy trading presents avenues for revenue generation, environmental stewardship, and social equity. By encouraging the adoption of renewable energy, improving energy efficiency, bolstering community resilience, and stimulating innovation, energy trading platforms have the capacity to generate positive impacts on society, economy, and the environment. These platforms pave the way for a more sustainable and resilient global energy landscape, facilitating international cooperation and contributing to a brighter future for generations ahead.

As we journey through the dynamic terrain of energy trading, it's crucial to cultivate cooperation, creativity, and alliances among different sectors and stakeholders. This collaborative effort is essential to unleash the complete potential of renewable energy, fostering a future that's both sustainable and fair for generations ahead.

## GLOSSARY

blockchain:
: A secure, shared database where information is chronologically linked and tamperproof 

DAPP:
: Decentralized application 

deficient:
: Not having enough of the demanded material or quality; someone with deficit 

deficit:
: The amount needed of something to meet demand 

microgrid:
: A small-scale energy network that can function either connected to the main power grid or independently 

prosumer:
: An individual who consumes and produces value 

PV:
: Photovoltaic 

SDK:
: Software development kit 

surplus:
: An excess in production or supply 

VPP:
: Virtual power plant

# 4A Comprehensive Review on Artificial IntelligenceMachine Learning Models for Environmental Sustainability

R. M. Bhavadharini, P. Mercy Rajaselvi Beaulah, and R. Krithiga

DOI: [10.1201/9781003497189-4](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003497189-4)

## INTRODUCTION

A sustainable environment is required to conserve natural resources and to maintain ecological balance in our planet's natural environment. As the population grows on earth, the need for excessive production for food and other industries results in energy usage and damages to natural resources. In order to live long and healthy lives, human society as a whole needs clean water to drink, clean air to breathe, and locations free of hazardous materials \[[1](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_1)\]. The rapid advancements in the domain of artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning (ML), and the Internet of Things (IoT) have triggered massive transformations across various domains, including resource management, healthcare monitoring and management, environmental monitoring, climate prediction, autonomous vehicles, supply chain management, agriculture, and so on. Deep usage of AI and IoT leads to innovative solutions and practices across various sectors.

One of the abundant and vital natural resources for all lives on earth is water. The 17 sustainable development goals (SDG) adopted by all members of the United Nations aim to improve the global environment for healthy human life on earth. SDG 6, titled “Clean Water and Sanitation”, describes access to clean and safe water as a fundamental requirement for human well-being \[[2](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_2)\]. Urbanization and industrialization have led to water contamination, which poses a big challenge to well-being on earth. The combination of growing population, industrialization, urbanization, increased vehicles, pollution, and climate change makes water a scarce and more contaminated resource. Water pollutants move into water bodies like ponds, lakes, seas, and oceans and dissolve or are sedimented on the beds or sediments. The polluted water is deteriorated in its quality and becomes unsafe for consumption. The various pollutants of water include viruses, bacteria, pharmaceutical products, nitrates, fecal waste, and plastics \[[3](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_3)\]. According to a new report on inequalities in access to water by the World Health Organization (WHO) and United Nations Children's Fund (UNICEF), around 4.2 billion people worldwide need adequate sanitation facilities, and that around 2.2 billion people suffer from inadequate access to safely managed drinking water \[[4](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_4)\]. Polluted and unsafe water can cause and transmit diseases such as cholera, diarrhea, dysentery, hepatitis A, typhoid, and polio. Inadequate, or unavailable, water and sanitation facilities severely expose human communities to avoidable health hazards. Therefore, guaranteeing and safeguarding the water quality for human consumption is a significant challenge to address.

Just as water quality monitoring ensures safe water for human consumption, air quality monitoring guarantees safe unpolluted air for well-being. Air pollution poses one of the largest risks to human health. Air pollution endangers around seven million people's health, according to WHO. It is a major risk factor for many illnesses, including respiratory system disorders, skin infections, heart difficulties, bronchitis, asthma, and diseases of the throat and eyes. It is a major risk factor for many illnesses, including respiratory system disorders, skin infections, heart difficulties, bronchitis, asthma, and diseases of the throat and eyes \[[5](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_5)\]. Crop health monitoring and growth depend heavily on soil quality monitoring and weather prediction capabilities. By analyzing soil nutrients, available water resources, and prevailing weather conditions, framers can plan crop selection, seed quality, crop disease monitoring, irrigation processes, fertilization, harvesting, storage, and marketing and thus massively improving crop yields. Precision agriculture (PA) makes use of smart technologies in improving crop yields and assisting the farmers in decision making. PA aims to reduce labor time and increase crop productivity, thus impacting a country's economic growth. PA enables farmers to remotely monitor the data to make better decisions for maintaining sustainable environments. Wildlife monitoring is another domain considered in this chapter. It plays an important role in maintaining a sustainable environment. It helps in maintaining biodiversity conservation, mitigating human–animal conflict, high-valued species protection, ecological parameter balancing, and species disease surveillance. AI plays significantly in wildlife monitoring to collect the data automatically and to process the data. The different forms of data from wildlife sources can include camera traps, satellite images, acoustic signals, and invasive and noninvasive sensor readings.

Disasters caused by climatic change and other ecological factors are very hard to predict and manage due to their uncertainty. Nowadays, the state-of-the-art technological improvements and advancements can aid in prediction, mitigation, and management of natural or man-made disasters. IoT, the rapidly growing framework, can remotely monitor the metrological, hydrological, and environmental parameters, and support in real-time data collection and predictive analysis. Risk identification, disaster preparedness, emergency response, resource allocation, reaction planning, and disaster recovery are the first steps in the process.

This review chapter provides insights about the importance of AI and ML techniques used in the literature surveyed from the perspectives of the following aspects:

1.  Different ML/Deep Learning (DL) algorithms evaluated in surveyed literature papers
    
2.  Various water/air/soil quality parameters assessed by different ML/DL models implemented in the literature survey
    
3.  Comparison of performance results achieved by ML/DL models explored in the literature
    

## METHODOLOGY

AI and ML models are capable of processing large volumes of data sourced from diverse origins such as sensors, satellites, and field measurements. They can easily identify the patterns and recognize anomalies in the data source. They are capable of facilitating real-time monitoring and decision support processes. These technologies can be easily incorporated with IoT, such as sensors and actuators deployed across various domains to collect and process data involving reduced man power and computing resources. As a powerful tool in data analysis, ML is widely deployed to analyze and identify patterns and make appropriate predictions based on enormous amounts of data or big data generated through different types of sources. Supervised learning algorithms, unsupervised learning algorithms, and reinforcement algorithms are the classifications of the ML algorithms. In order to analyze the connectivity and relationship between the dependent and independent variables, supervised learning algorithms are used. They produce predictive models that can predict the output based on the given input. The applications based on regression and classification use supervised learning methods.

In contrast to supervised learning algorithms, unsupervised learning algorithms are used when the output label is not known. The input data is classified based on the similarity characteristics into the output class. Unsupervised learning algorithms are used in applications involving clustering. Algorithms like principal component analysis (PCA) and K-means are a couple of the commonly known unsupervised learning algorithms. Furthermore, another category of machine learning methods is called reinforcement learning, which describes a machine's capacity to generalize and provide accurate answers to issues that it hasn’t been taught.

Most of the literature papers surveyed in this chapter use the following architecture models in their study. These research papers collected and processed data for various purposes like water quality assessment, air quality assessment, precision agriculture, wildlife monitoring, and disaster management. The architecture shown in [Figure 4.1](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_1) shows the flow of methodology used in most of the research works considered here.

![FIGURE 4.1 System architecture of the methodology.](images/fig4_1.jpg)

[FIGURE 4.1](#chapter4.xhtml#b4fig4_1) System architecture of the methodology.

The general system architecture shown in [Figure 4.1](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_1) uses the data collected from various sources such as publicly available datasets or real-time data. The data type includes numerical, categorical, text, sound, and images. The ML model is trained and verified by dividing the data into training and test sets at a predetermined ratio and then checking how well the model performs when it processes the unseen data. The model's performance is evaluated through a number of performance metrics like accuracy, confusion matrix, area under region of curve, precision, mean squared error (MSE), root mean squared error (RMSE), and R²(R-Squared).

### AI/ML/DL in Water Quality Monitoring

Authors in \[[6](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_6)\] proposed an Enhanced Automated Machine Learning Model (EAMLM) to assess water quality. Using temperature, pH, Total Dissolved Solid (TDS), and LDR (light dependent) sensors, they gathered data on the water quality parameters. The machine learning model is trained on this dataset of water quality parameters, and its performance is assessed using performance measures. AI/ML algorithms and models have substantial prospects in monitoring, predicting, and classifying the water quality indicators due to the rapid growth in data volume. Authors in \[[7](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_7)\] evaluated the ML classifiers to classify the quality classes of the monitored water samples according to their Water Quality Index (WQI). More complicated nonlinear issues can be effectively solved using data-driven machine learning models. Deployment and evaluation of distinct machine learning models for treating different water samples collected from surface water, groundwater, drinking water, and wastewater was covered in \[[8](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_8)\]. Monitoring drinking water quality is essential in ensuring the safety of human society. Authors of \[[9](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_9)\] proposed an Artificial Neural Network (ANN) for predicting water quality, and achieved an accuracy of 0.96 using a small feature set; it outperforms existing models. In the same way, the proposed ANN also performed well for water consumption prediction, and achieved better performance than existing complex DL models such as convolutional neural network (CNN), Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM), and gated recurrent units (GRU). Researchers of \[[10](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_10)\] utilized and evaluated M5 Model Tree (MT), Multivariate Adaptive Regression Spline (MARS), Gene Expression Programming (GEP), and Evolutionary Polynomial Regression (EPR) to process Landsat 8 OLI-TIRS images for the purpose of water quality parameters assessment from river water samples. The proposed work \[[11](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_11)\] explored Principal Component Analysis with Artificial Neural Network for modeling. ANN with raw data, ANN with Principal Component (PC) prior to rotation, and ANN with PC following varimax rotation were all tested. Out of three ANN models, ANN A performed better in predicting water quality. Various ML models were evaluated to assess the water quality class (WQC) and the authors proved that Bagging Classifier and Multilayer Perceptron (MLP) outperformed all other ML models considered in this research work \[[12](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_12)\].

The research in \[[13](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_13)\] focused on LSTM deep neural network to assess the quality parameters of drinking water within an IoT environment. In \[[14](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_14)\], using physical factors as features, the authors employed various ML models to forecast and assess the groundwater quality parameters, hence enhancing the informed decision in agricultural practices. Adaboost and Random Forest (RF) prediction performances are better than other models in their study to predict groundwater quality. In \[[15](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_15)\], a DL-based forecasting system was developed to forecast groundwater quality; this DL model has outperformed other ML models considered for comparison in their work. For the purpose of forecasting ten irrigation water quality (IWQ) parameters dissolved in surface water, eight machine learning models have been evaluated and verified in \[[16](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_16)\]. In order to assess the river water quality, \[[17](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_17)\] evaluated four standalone and 12 hybrid data-mining methods. The model presented in \[[18](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_18)\] used LSTM and CNN integration to assess and forecast the temporal and spatial patterns seen in the data on water quality. Authors also evaluated the CNN model's hybrid, the CNN–LSTM model, for predicting water quality parameters, known as dissolved oxygen (DO) and chlorophyll-a (Chl-a). [Table 4.1](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_1) presents various ML algorithms used for water quality assessment and their performance results.

### AI/ML/DL in Air Quality Monitoring

The sources of pollution emissions increase with the demand of transportation and energy due to increasing population. Human beings can lead better lifestyles through monitoring the environment for pollution and controlling pollution-related diseases. One of the main issues facing cities today is air pollution, the reduction of which is also the main goal of smart cities. Air pollution results from the discharge of particular substances, such as gases, particles, and biological molecules, into the atmosphere in hazardous or excessive proportions. These high emissions have clear effects on the environment, harming crops and resulting in diseases and population declines among other living things. There are three types of air pollutants: gases, liquid droplets, and solid particles \[[19](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_19)\]. In the study \[[20](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_20)\], a novel early warning system for air quality with Whale Optimization Algorithm (WOA) was used in conjunction with a system known as Improved Complete Ensemble Empirical Mode Decomposition with Adaptive Noise (ICEEMDAN) to monitor air pollutants such as Particulate Matter with a diameter of 2.5 micrometers or less (PM[2.5]), Particulate Matter with a diameter of 10 micrometers or less (PM[10]), nitrogen dioxide (NO[2]), sulfur dioxide (SO[2]), carbon monoxide (CO), and ozone (O[3]). An LSTM Deep Neural Network (DNN)-based model was developed and used as an underlying prediction technique to monitor the air quality in \[[21](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_21)\]. A comprehensive air quality forecasting model for smart city uses was proposed, employing two deep learning models and four machine learning approaches. The DL models included LSTM Ada Boost, and the ML models included Support Vector Regression (SVR), RF, KNN, and MLP regressor for tracking pollution markers including PM[2.5], PM[10], O[3], and CO in \[[22](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_22)\]. LSTM has proven to outperform other ML models used in this research work. Authors in \[[23](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_23)\] demonstrated that RF Regression (RFR) has outperformed DT, GBR, and MLP models in their study for pollution prediction with varying sizes of datasets and locations having various environmental characteristics. Study in \[[24](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_24)\] used regression model SVR and RFR for predicting and analyzing the Air Quality Index (AQI) and these models were evaluated with publicly available datasets. SVR was used by the authors in \[[19](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_19)\] to forecast particle and pollutant levels as well as the AQI.

The most accurate predictions for pollutant concentrations, including PM[2.5], SO[2], NO[2], O[3], and CO, were obtained using SVR with Radial Basis Function (RBF), along with the hourly AQI. Researchers in \[[25](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_25)\] explored various Machine Learning Regressor models to predict PM10 and PM2.5 concentration in the air. They collected historical data for PM concentration levels along with environmental factors to compare various ML models. The ML models Multi-Linear Regression (MLR), decision tree (DT), K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), RF, and XGBoost were evaluated for analyzing the spatial-temporal parameters in \[[26](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_26)\] for PM[2.5] prediction. For PM[2.5] modeling, the LSTM deep learning model performed better than XGBoost regression, with its achieved performance result of R² value as 0.89. A predictive model based on artificial neural network (ANN) was employed to forecast the concentration levels of SO[2] and particulate matter \[[27](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_27)\]. ANN achieved the results as RMSE values of 0.0128 for SO[2] and 0.0001 PM[2.5]. Authors in \[[28](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_28)\] used ML models like Seasonal Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (SARIMA), SVM, and LSTM to predict AQI. [Table 4.3](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_3) shows the ML models used in the research works surveyed in this chapter along with their results. ### AI/ML in Soil Quality Monitoring and Precision Agriculture

A unique algorithm utilizing a multi-class SVM with a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) with the fruit fly optimization approach (MSVM-DAG-FFO) was proposed in \[[29](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_29)\]. With a 0.973 accuracy rate, this model outperformed SVM (0.932), SVM kernel (0.922), and decision tree (0.914).ML algorithms were used to examine the information gathered and produce crop recommendations based on trends found. These suggestions support effective resource use in agriculture by optimizing crop development and maximizing yield potential. Artificial intelligence helps farmers in choosing the suitable seed for a certain set of weather conditions. Along with it, AI also offers meteorological forecast information, and AI-powered solutions will accelerate a product's time to reach market, enhance crop and soil quality, and allow farmers to increase crop yields with fewer resources \[[30](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_30)\]. Authors \[[31](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_31)\] used KNN imputer to impute missing data values in the soil dataset. They tried with seven foundational classifiers and eight ensemble classifiers for soil classification and they achieved good performance with XGBoost and LightGBM classifiers.

In \[[32](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_32)\], authors developed and evaluated DNN and ML models on both dry land and paddy fields to observe soil quality. The RF model outperformed all ML models with R² = 0.91 for soil quality prediction of paddy fields and with R² = 0.90 for dry land. In \[[33](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_33)\], the use of the genetic algorithm (GA) optimizer in conjunction with extreme gradient boosting regression (XGBR) for feature selection and optimization was suggested and assessed. The proposed XGBR-GA model achieved the maximum performance of R² = 0.891; and RMSE = 0.875%. Various ML and DL models were evaluated to assess the soil quality in \[[34](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_34)\]. Convolutional neural networks (CNNs) were used in a portable smartphone-based machine vision system that was proposed in \[[35](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_35)\]. This model used one block for feature extraction and a second block for classification with SVM classifier. The authors of \[[36](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_36)\] used optical and thermal sensors to collect soil moisture, which was then analyzed using machine learning (ML) models such as RF, SVM, ANN, and elastic net regression (EN).

The ML-driven models were created to improve soil salinity inversion precision and accuracy, and they have produced the most promising results, with R² = 0.801, 0.624, 0.613 and RMSE = 0.686, 0.875, 0.918, for the RF, SVM, and BP models, respectively \[[37](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_37)\]. The prominent ML models, partial least squares regression (PLSR), K nearest neighbor (KNN), and RFR were trained and tested with Unmanned Ariel Vehicle (UAV)-based multimodal data to compute the amount of moisture in the soil in a field of maize at various irrigation levels. Study in \[[38](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_38), [39](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_39)\] investigated estimation models based on ML for salt-affected irrigation regions in order to support the prompt and precise monitoring of soil water–salt within agricultural lands in real time. [Table 4.5](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_5) summarizes a number of ML models used in the reference papers considered in this chapter. ### AI/ML in Wildlife Monitoring

The acoustic signals and the images of the animals are captured and processed to monitor the animals in real time. Researchers use ML/DL models for feature extraction and classification tasks for classifying the animal species for ecological conservation, animal tracking and health monitoring, and protecting human beings from animal attacks. Some of the monitoring systems use the alert messaging system, for triggering messages to a group of registered mobile numbers once the animal is detected and identified. The ML/DL models are also used to monitor and to record in climatic parameters. These real-time parameters are used to predict the intensity of forest fires, and assist to avoid significant losses caused by forest fires. The CNN architecture implemented in \[[40](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_40)\] achieved a recognition accuracy of more than 96% to detect different animals’ species from the images. The automatic classification of wildlife species from camera trap photos using convolutional neural networks with the ResNet-18 architecture has achieved with 98% accuracy \[[41](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_41)\]. Two new frameworks for automatic image recognition using deep learning were implemented in \[[42](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_42)\] and they achieved an accuracy of 98.05% for binary classification and an accuracy of 90.32% for multiclassification. An ML-assisted tool, MegaDetector, was used to tag animals in the video feed using bounding boxes, and they demonstrated that tagging images with ML-based bounding boxes has been faster than tagging by unassisted \[[43](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_43)\]. A biodiversity assessment model using an ML approach was proposed in \[[44](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_44)\]. This model captured birds’ audio and extracted the features using Mel-Frequency Cepstral Coefficients (MFCC). Then they used a machine learning model, Multilayer Perceptron (MLP), to classify the bird species. With 0.92 specificity, 0.74 accuracy, and 0.74 sensitivity, this approach produced better results. Authors in \[[45](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_45)\] used ResNet50 architecture to construct the model and Transfer Learning to accelerate training processes with weights of the pre-trained model on the ImageNet dataset. This novel model makes it possible to automatically label animal and bird species from the camera trap. Authors studied two deep learning models, CNN and ResNet, to automate the process of detecting animal species \[[46](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_46)\]. The proposed ResNet architecture obtained an overall accuracy of 99.18% in animal identification. The CNN model in this study achieved the highest overall accuracy and Kappa as 84.55% and 0.79, respectively. ### AI/ML in Flood Monitoring

Authors in \[[47](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_47)\] employed Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction for sensor data sourced from various sensors and an ANN model to forecast the flood-related parameters. ANN in this research work achieved an accuracy of 94.2% and ROC = 0.9807. The model in \[[48](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_48)\] analyzed the flood sensor data to predict and classify the severity class of floods on the river side. They deployed and assessed the performance of many ML models individually and ensembled models for this purpose. The flood severity class has three predictions in this work: normal, abnormal, and dangerous water levels. During the training and validation phase, the ensemble LSTM+ RF classifier had an accuracy of 0.997; however, in the testing phase utilizing unknown data, the accuracy was 0.811. In the work \[[49](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_49)\], a deep learning–based control system has been demonstrated to observe and analyze the flow of flood from the dam gates. Authors have evaluated the performance of CNN, ANN, and SVM. According to the results, the CNN algorithm-based classification system for estimating available space at the tank operates effectively and accurately with an accuracy of 85.53, whereas the other two models have an accuracy of ANN = 82.89 and SVM = 80.26. In \[[50](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_50)\], the flood situation data undergoes a classification process using extreme gradient boosting (XGBOOST) and gated recurrent unit (GRU) algorithms; XGBOOST showed R² = 0.92 and GRU with R² = 0.96. An ANN model was used in \[[51](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_51)\] to process the data collected by water level and rainfall sensor nodes. They exploited LSTM for short-term prediction of flood water levels and achieved an R² value as 0.976.

### AI/ML in Earthquake Monitoring

In order to predict earthquakes, a unique Bayesian belief model technique was applied in \[[52](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_52)–[53](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_53)\]. Moreover, the amount of seismic activity in the cloud layer was predicted utilizing the Adaptive Neuro-Fuzzy Inference System (ANFIS) method. Based on the extensive simulation results, the suggested framework performs with higher classification performance, achieving precision of 92.52%, sensitivity of 91.72%, and specificity of 91.01%. Authors have evaluated various ML models and nonlinear models, and the XGB model has performed well with a classification accuracy of 98.59% to predict earthquake intensity \[[53](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_53)\]. In order to quickly calculate the EQ parameters (magnitude and position) in IoT-based EEW, a unique deep learning model combining AE and CNN, dubbed 3S-AE-CNN, was developed. ## RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

This chapter considers accuracy, precision, MSE, RMSE, and R² for comparing results achieved in the literature surveyed and they are tabulated in [Tables 4.1](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_1), [4.3](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_3), [4.5](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_5), [4.7](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_7), and [4.8](#chapter4.xhtml#t4_8). These parameters are described next.

### Accuracy

Accuracy is a metric used for evaluating the performance of classification model. It is determined by dividing correct prediction with total number of predictions of the model.

  
$$\\text{Accuracy} = \\frac{TP + TN}{TP + FP + TN + FN}$$  
(4.1)

where *TP* = True Positives, *TN* = True Negatives, *FP* = False Positives, and *FN* = False Negatives.

### Precision

Precision is measure that determines how often the ML model correctly predicts the positive classes in the total correctly predicted classes. Precision is given as in Equation 2.

  
$$\\text{Precision} = \\frac{TP}{TP + FP}$$  
(4.2)

### MSE (Mean Squared Error)

MSE is a metric used to evaluate the ML model's predictive power. The average squared difference between the expected and actual output values is used to calculate MSE. Equation 3 provides the MSE, where *y* represents the actual output, *ŷ* the anticipated output, and *n* the number of predictions utilized in the machine learning model.

  
$$\\text{MSE} = \\frac{1}{n}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n}{(y - \\hat{y})}^{2}$$  
(4.3)

### RMSE (Root Mean Squared Error)

RMSE is a predictive metric used to analyze and evaluate the performance of an ML model. It calculates the average magnitude of the error and deviation from the actual parameter. The better model has an RMSE value closer to 0 and is given by Equation 4.

  
$$\\text{RMSE} = \\sqrt{\\frac{1}{n}{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 0}^{n}\\left( {y - \\hat{y}} \\right)^{2}}}$$  
(4.4)

### R² (R Squared)

The percentage of the dependent variable's variance that the independent variable can explain for is called the R squared. R squared is given by Equation 5.

  
$$\\text{R}^{2} = \\frac{SS\_{regressiom}}{SS\_{total}}$$  
(4.5)

Where SS[regression] represents Sum of Squares in regression modes and SS[total] represents total sum of squares.

In [Figure 4.2](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_2), the performance achievement of various ML/DL algorithms is represented on the basis of the accuracy achieved by proposed models in the reference papers \[[7](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_7), [9](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_9), [12](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_12)\]. Linear Regression, Multi Linear Regression, CATBoost algorithms achieve better performance than other models considered in the performance comparison to assess the water quality parameters. [Figure 4.3](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_3) compares the performance results achieved in terms of R² for air quality assessment parameters. It is clearly evident from [Figure 4.3](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_3) that GBR \[[25](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_25)\], SVM-RBF \[[26](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_26)\], and LSTM \[[28](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_28)\] are outperforming other models considered in the literature with an achieved result of R² = 0.91, 0.89, and 0.89, respectively. In order to assess the soil quality parameters, various ML/DL models are compared in terms of R², and [Figure 4.4](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_4) depicts the comparison. The models RF \[[32](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_32)\], DNN \[[32](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_32)\], XGBR-GA \[[33](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_33)\] outperform the other ML/DL models with achieved R² values of 0.998, 0.998, and 0.89, respectively. [Figure 4.5](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_5) compares the models used in wildlife monitoring. These systems take input data as acoustic signals, animal images captured through PiCamera, and sensor data. The results in [Figure 4.5](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_5) show that ResNet \[[46](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_46)\] achieves an accuracy of 99.18% and performs better than other architectures considered for comparison. This review article considers flood prediction and earthquake prediction for the disaster management field. [Figure 4.6](#chapter4.xhtml#f4_6) compares the models, and it is shown that ensemble LSTM+ RF \[[48](#chapter4.xhtml#r4_48)\] classifier reaches the maximum result of 99.7% accuracy.

## CONCLUSION

Sustainable environment monitoring plays a vital role in safeguarding our environment for healthy and peaceful lives for human beings. The modern technological developments in the fields of AI/ML and IoT devised and implemented many intelligent solutions in real-time monitoring, collection, storage, and processing data. They enable quick and knowledgeable decision-making processes with the prediction and classification systems/models. This chapter aimed to present recent advancement and deployments of AI and ML models in sustainable environment monitoring. The chapter presented the contribution of AI and ML models in the realms of water quality, air quality, soil quality, wildlife monitoring, and disaster management. This comprehensive review presented various sensors deployed for data collection and data processing in these domains. Also, different ML/DL algorithms used in these fields were presented, along with their achieved results.
