# 8Sustainable Smart Cities in African Digital Space

Basil Osayin Daudu, Goddy Uwa Osimen, and Esther Besty Anaiye

DOI: [10.1201/9781003497189-8](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003497189-8)

## INTRODUCTION

One remarkable factor that has distinguished advanced countries from developing and underdeveloped countries is technological advancement, giving the advanced countries an edge over others. Through the impact of technological advancements, citizens’ lives are improved upon; the economy is boosted for the good of all; and communication among individuals, groups, countries, and continents is strengthened, thereby, making the world a global village. The African digital space houses smart cities created to improve the welfare of the African people. Sustainability, as Moir, Moonen, and Clark \[[1](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_1)\] asserted, “is no longer the main prism through which thinking about the future of cities takes place but rather the introduction of smart cities has become the most popular formulation for the future city”. Recently, the discourse on smart cities has gained utmost importance as urban centres nationwide seek innovative solutions to the complicated problems of the 21st century, and rapid urbanisation, demographic shifts, and economic development, amongst others, are considered the key drivers of progressive smart cities in the African continent.

It is not news that the world today is technologically inclined, and with the concern of urban populations predicted to increase shortly, African cities are faced with accommodation problems and essential delivery systems and sustainable development for their citizens. In this regard, the idea of smart cities paved the way for solving the multifaceted problems of urbanisation and at the same time, using digital technologies as tools to better the lives of urban residents. On this note, the significant influx of people into cities has strained urban infrastructure, necessitating the transformation of cities into smarter, more efficient, and sustainable environments. This entails prioritising technological advancements in infrastructure development across sectors such as construction, energy, water, and waste management \[[2](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_2)\]. Despite the socio-economic, infrastructural, and institutional challenges on the ground, African cities have recorded outstanding performance in making the best out of the available technological resources to meet the needs of the locals. As Munene \[[3](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_3)\] noted, African cities have started adopting smart urbanism and innovation, exemplified by initiatives like mobile payment systems for financial inclusion and crowdsourced mapping platforms for community involvement in urban planning. Nevertheless, unlocking the complete benefits of smart cities in Africa necessitates collective endeavours to overcome obstacles such as restricted technology access, insufficient infrastructure, and governance deficiencies.

African cities are poised to be the primary engines of future growth and are expected to accommodate nearly 60% of the continent's population \[[4](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_4)\]. This perspective is reinforced by the African Union's Agenda 2063, emphasising the pivotal role African cities will play in the continent's future development. South Africa is said to lead the way in smart city technology adoption in Africa and recognises the comprehensive benefits of implementing smart cities, including enhancing the quality of life for residents. So to speak, at least nine cities in South Africa are actively implementing smart city concepts, including Buffalo City, the City of Johannesburg, City of Tshwane, City of Cape Town, Ekurhuleni, eThekwini, Mangaung, Msunduzi, and Nelson Mandela Bay. Among these, Johannesburg, Cape Town, and Durban are at the forefront of implementing various smart city initiatives and technological solutions \[[5](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_5)\]. Initiatives have been formulated and implemented to improve the welfare of Africans in smart cities: in Cape Town, South Africa (smart metering for water and electricity, traffic management systems, and digital platforms for citizen engagement); Kigali, Rwanda (projects such as smart street lighting, a bus rapid transit system with real-time tracking, and a digital marketplace for services); Nairobi, Kenya (smart bus system, digital payment systems for public transportation, renewable energy projects and digital inclusion programs); Lagos, Nigeria (initiatives such as the deployment of traffic management systems, smart waste bins with sensors, and the development of a smart city master plan); and Accra, Ghana (projects such as a digitised land registry system, smart street lighting, and the implementation of e-governance solutions).

The emergence of smart cities across Africa, therefore, is a promising solution to the difficulties associated with rapid urbanisation, facilitated by smart technologies and innovations. What do smart cities entail? How progressive are the smart cities in Africa? What are the likely challenges in African smart cities and how can they be dealt with? All these are some of the issues to be dealt with in this chapter.

## THEMATIC REVIEW OF SMART CITY

Different connotations have been given to the concept of a smart city. A truly smart city places its citizens at the forefront, recognising them as its most valuable asset. Throughout the entire process of planning a smart city, from inception to completion, citizens and communities should play a central role. Prioritising people entails creating urban plans and designing spaces that cater to the needs of the population \[[6](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_6)\]. This principle should permeate all aspects of smart city planning. A smart city is also seen as a concept, means, action, and state \[[7](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_7)\]. Smart city as a concept here refers to underlying principles such as safety and security, equity and inclusiveness, resourcefulness and resilience, integration and inclusiveness, green growth, liveable environments, innovation and transformation, resource conservation, and public and private sector participation). A smart city encompasses planning, development, and management/operation (means) to address challenges and create new value (action). Ultimately, a smart city aspires to achieve a sustainable society (state).

A smart city is an urban area that boasts advanced infrastructure, sustainable real estate, robust communication systems, and market viability. It relies heavily on information technology as the primary infrastructure for delivering essential services to its residents \[[8](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_8)\]. This involves the use of various technological platforms, such as automated sensor networks and data centres. Economic development in a smart city is characterised by sustainability and gradual growth driven by market forces like supply and demand. The effectiveness of smart cities is heavily reliant on technological advancements. On this note, Kitchin \[[9](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_9)\] commented on the various domains of smart cities and their respective technologies: domains such as government (e-government systems, online transactions, city operating systems, performance management systems, urban dashboards), security and emergency services (centralised control rooms, digital surveillance; predictive policing, coordinated emergency response), transport (intelligent transport systems, integrated ticketing, smart travel cards, bikeshare, real-time passenger information, smart parking, logistics management, transport apps, dynamic road signs), energy (smart grids; smart meters, energy usage apps, smart lighting, waste \[compactor bins and dynamic routing/collection\]), environment (IoT sensor networks, e.g., pollution, noise, weather, land movement, flood management), dynamically responsive interventions (e.g., automated flood defences), buildings (building management systems, sensor networks), and homes (smart meters, app-controlled smart appliances).

A smart city as the integration of intelligence, political strategy, and infrastructure to deliver comprehensive urban services. Developing a smart city involves combining and strategising factors such as smart mobility, smart living, and smart economy to achieve an environmentally friendly, sustainable, and intelligent urban environment \[[10](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_10)\]. A smart city also utilises urban informatics and technology to enhance service efficiency. Information and Communication Technology (ICT) enables city officials to directly engage with the community and urban infrastructure, monitoring city developments and striving for an improved quality of life \[[11](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_11)\]. Real-time monitoring systems integrated with sensors collect data from citizens and devices, which are then processed and analysed. This data-driven approach is crucial for addressing inefficiencies within the city.

One obvious benefit of a smart city is the level of government response to the needs and well-being of citizens through positive interaction between the government and citizens, aided by technology-driven policies and services. To this effect, the techno-utopian paradigm explicates the contextual clues and reflects on smart cities merely as an ideal urban situation where technology fixes every foreseeable urban problem, the neoliberalists perceive a smart city as a technologically enhanced situation in which digital versions of essential services are scaled up to promote capitalist ‘takeover’ and administering of the city. It fosters a globalised business ecosystem, a business-dominated technological interest, rather than radical and comprehensive urban planning \[[12](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_12)\]. On his part, Watson \[[13](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_13)\] warns Africa and India of the consequences of envisioning a revolutionary city against the foreground of ‘fantasy cities’, such as Dubai and Singapore. Although Grossi and Pianezzi \[[14](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_14)\] defended the view of a smart city being a techno-utopian system, whilst at the same time it acts as a generator of a collective imaginary, it is believed that a smart city promotes the shared interests of business elites and devolves the city development attention away from urgent urban problems, such as urbanisation.

A smart city is the digital fusion of information system components, aimed at gathering digital data and analysing it instantaneously. This process allows for the continuous monitoring and management of city infrastructure, facilitating efficient resource allocation \[[15](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_15)\]. Ultimately, this approach enhances service delivery and enhances the quality of life for citizens. Thus, four significant challenges hinder the realisation of smart cities of many developing countries: (i) the absence of a comprehensive historical account detailing the evolution of smart city knowledge, key issues, and implementation strategies; (ii) the prevalence of generic examples and similar narratives in literature discussing smart city issues; (iii) limited in-depth exploration of smart city development and scarce comparative research elucidating its progress across different regions of the world; and (iv) minimal involvement of various stakeholders in the actualisation of smart cities. Addressing these challenges collectively poses a complex task, requiring nuanced approaches \[[12](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_12)\]. To some scholars, the concept of a smart city is grounded in the integration of intelligent infrastructures and ICTs with human connections. Sustainability (enhancing the relationship between the city and its environment while embracing the green economy principle), smartness (fostering an economy and governance structure that are contextually aware), and inclusiveness (promoting an economy with high employment rates that fosters social and territorial cohesion) are therefore considered as the three key axes that city development should prioritise; a city, therefore, achieves smartness when it achieves sustainable economic growth through investments in human and social capital, modern transportation and communication infrastructure, and a participatory government that effectively manages natural resources \[[16](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_16)\].

Governments and public agencies, so to speak, now employ the term ‘smart city’ as a pivotal concept in contemporary urban development initiatives and policies aimed at achieving sustainable development, robust economic growth, and enhanced quality of life \[[17](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_17)\]. A city becomes smart when investments in human and social capital, alongside both traditional (transport) and modern (ICT) communication infrastructure, drive sustainable economic growth and high-quality living standards, while also managing natural resources wisely through participatory engagement \[[18](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_18)\]. In essence, a smart city not only possesses ICT infrastructure but also utilises it in ways that positively impact the local community and environment. Therefore, a smart city is founded on technology but directed towards achieving specific outcomes. Its primary goal is to enhance the quality of life for citizens and enhance the efficiency and effectiveness of services delivered by local government bodies and businesses \[[5](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_5)\]. Internet connectivity is vital, and it plays a crucial role in facilitating communication between municipalities and residents \[[19](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_19)\]. In analysing data, six predominant themes emerged, representing challenges hindering the implementation of smart city initiatives in small and rural municipalities: lack of infrastructure, internet connectivity, technology, skilled workforce, budget constraints, and sustainable energy \[[20](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_20)\].

Smart cities are complex configurations of technology, organisations, and people that enable data about the city to be collected and flow to those who plan and deliver services in cities, leading to better urban outcomes for citizens and businesses. Platforms are fundamental to smart cities as they facilitate the integration of diverse data sources for access and utilisation by various stakeholders. With the growing volume, speed, and diversity of data (i.e., Big Data), platforms play an increasingly vital role in converting disparate data into actionable information and insights \[[21](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_21)\]. Platforms serve as the foundation of smart cities, enabling the aggregation, analysis, and delivery of essential information to city planners and service providers. Economic perspectives, particularly market mechanisms for data and related services, are crucial in understanding value exchange within these platforms in [Figure 8.1](#chapter8.xhtml#f8_1). Such mechanisms can be explicit, as seen in open data repositories, or implicit, where third-party contributors derive value from data in ways not always apparent to data providers and platform users \[[22](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_22), [23](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_23)\].

![FIGURE 8.1 Major features of a smart city.](images/fig8_1.jpg)

[FIGURE 8.1](#chapter8.xhtml#b8fig8_1) Major features of a smart city.

Smart economy, smart people, smart governance, smart mobility, smart environment, and smart living are regarded as the major or the defining features of a smart city \[[5](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_5)\]. In this chapter, therefore, a smart city is taken to mean a technologically and densely populated human settlement.

## ORIGIN OF SMART CITIES

The term ‘smart city’ emerged in the early 1990s to describe the application of technology and innovation in urban development. Specifically, during this period, researchers focused on cities and their integration of Information Technology (IT). The concept of the smart city transitioned into the digital city in 1994 in Amsterdam. By 1997, approximately 2000 virtual urban areas were identified globally \[[24](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_24)\]. These virtual cities introduced local ICT networks, facilitating the emergence of cyber-based (virtual) communities. They served as electronic and web-based representations of real urban areas, hosted via the World Wide Web (WWW). Virtual cities were recognised as early attempts to utilise the internet to support grassroots democracy, urban promotion, internet-based municipal operations, and social development within cities. However, a lack of citizen feedback was noted. Subsequently, the virtual community emerged in 1998, enabling communication among individuals based on shared norms. This virtual community network had limited digitalisation scope, primarily serving its associated community, with outsiders having no direct access to the community network.

The internet, in conjunction with city systems and the WWW, was utilised to establish city websites offering alternative smart services, such as information retrieval and official and general communication. These two approaches to smart cities envision urban spaces as either connected communities or two- or three-dimensional virtual environments \[[25](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_25)\]. The first concept of a smart city was considered in Dubai in 1999. Another prominent digital city system was developed in Kyoto in 1998, resulting in the creation of 2D and 3D spaces where resident interactions were captured through sensors, and their behaviours were animated. The digital city model evolved into what was known as an information city, characterised as an urban area where ICT serves as the primary driver for delivering innovative online services. This idea of an information city later transformed into the concept of the ubiquitous city, where data is accessible through embedded urban communications \[[26](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_26)\]. The intelligent city emphasises city performance, focusing on innovation in areas such as intelligence, creativity, originality, communal intelligence, and artificial intelligence. The idea of the ‘smart city’ arises in reaction to the numerous obstacles posed by the ongoing expansion of urban environments. Effective deployment of technology, notably the Internet of Things (IoT), emerges as a prominent feature of thriving smart cities \[[27](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_27)\].

Smart city models have evolved into increasingly complex environments capable of offering a broader range of services and facilitating scientific integration, which is the ability of technology to embed itself into social systems to achieve smart service delivery \[[28](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_28)\]. This integration spans from basic information dissemination to the implementation of intelligent systems, then progresses to systems addressing social and human concerns, and finally to ecological systems focused on sustainability. In contrast, city ecosystems are commonly understood as communities of interacting organisms and their environments, forming complex networks due to resource interdependencies \[[29](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_29)\]. An ecosystem can be conceptualised as “an interdependent social system of actors, organizations, material infrastructures, and symbolic resources” \[[30](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_30)\]. In this context, ecosystems, like other systems, consist of elements, interconnections, and a function/purpose, but they are unique in that their elements are intelligent, autonomous, adaptive agents often forming communities, and they adapt to changes such as the addition or removal of elements. Herein, four essential elements characterise ecosystems, viz. interaction/engagement, balance, loosely connected actors with shared goals, and self-organisation.

In excavating the concept of ‘smart city’ lies the concept of ‘cyberspace’. Cyberspace is a network between computer systems and the digital environment in Africa that serves as a miniature representation of worldwide cyberspace, facilitating interactions, activities, experiences, and associations among both young and adult populations. However, the lack of regulation in these interactions raises concerns \[[31](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_31)\]. The African Digital Rights Network has been established to enhance the digital landscape in Africa. It seeks to achieve this by providing an online platform that facilitates democratic expression, safeguards privacy and freedom of speech, expands digital access, promotes social media activism, develops civic technologies, advocates for legislation supporting digital rights, and advances digital security technologies \[[32](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_32)\]. These endeavours align with the principles of smart cities, which utilise technology and data-driven solutions to enhance efficiency, sustainability, mobility, safety, and overall quality of life for residents. A smart city is a community where investments in human and social capital, alongside traditional and modern communication infrastructure, contribute to sustainable economic development, improved quality of life, and responsible management of natural resources \[[33](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_33)\]. Similarly, smart cities are places where physical, digital, and human systems are effectively integrated within the built environment to foster a sustainable, prosperous, and inclusive future for residents.

As noted by Kumar and Barrett \[[34](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_34)\], the fundamental aim of smart cities is to utilise technology to create urban environments that are sustainable, efficient, and inclusive, with a focus on the well-being of their inhabitants. In Africa, smart cities are increasingly seen as pioneering solutions to urban challenges, aiming to enhance efficiency, sustainability, and inclusiveness. Cities such as Cairo, Algiers, Cape Town, Rabat, Nairobi, Lagos, Abuja, Tunis, and Accra are recognised as the most advanced cities in terms of smart initiatives on the African continent \[[35](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_35)\]. Several countries in Africa including South Africa, Namibia, Kenya, Egypt, Algeria, Morocco, Nigeria, and Ghana are at the forefront of promoting the smart city concept. These nations are embracing modernisation to enhance the well-being of their citizens and shape a future that harmonises innovation, humanity, and nature \[[36](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_36)\]. For instance, the development of Konza Technopolis in Kenya as a smart city involves integrating urban information and communication technology networks. Upon completion, Konza Technopolis is expected to accommodate more than 230,000 residents and professionals who will live and work within the smart city. This initiative serves as a flagship project under Kenya's Vision 2030 economic development plan, which is aimed at propelling the country to middle-income status by 2030. Another instance is South African President Cyril Ramaphosa's vision for a novel city concept in South Africa, emphasising the integration of IoT devices and smart technology. In 2021, Ramaphosa unveiled three new development projects, namely, Lanseria, Durban Aerotropolis, and Mooiklo of Mega-City, to establish them as exemplars of South African smart cities \[[27](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_27)\].

## SMART CITIES IN AFRICA

The development of smart cities across Africa has progressed through various stages, with the most recent model prioritising citizen co-creation, equity, and social advancement. Cities such as Cape Town in South Africa, Lagos in Nigeria, and Cairo in Egypt, alongside their counterparts in the Western world like Vienna and Vancouver, have adopted this citizen-centred approach to encourage community participation and tackle diverse social challenges \[[34](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_34)\].

The evolution of smart cities in Africa has been a multifaceted journey marked by various stages of development and implementation of technology-driven solutions to address urban challenges. The concept of smart cities began to gain traction globally in the early 2000s, driven by advancements in technology and the increasing recognition of the need for sustainable urban development. African cities, particularly those with growing economies and infrastructure needs, started exploring smart initiatives during this period. In the 2010s, African cities started implementing pilot projects and initiatives to test smart solutions in various urban sectors. These initiatives often focused on improving infrastructure, enhancing public services, and addressing environmental challenges. Cities like Cape Town, Johannesburg, Nairobi, and Lagos became early adopters of smart technologies in Africa. Recognising the potential benefits of smart cities, several African governments began to formulate national strategies and policies to promote the development of smart cities. For example, Rwanda launched its Smart Cities Master Plan in 2016, outlining a road map for transforming urban areas using technology and innovation. Kigali, the capital of Rwanda, serves as the country's economic hub, focusing on overcoming economic and social challenges through innovative approaches. The primary objective of Kigali's smart city initiative is to enhance the quality of life for its residents through knowledge dissemination and education. The city aims to attract top tech talent from across Africa, taking inspiration from the success of Silicon Valley in the United States.

The late 2010s saw increased involvement of the private sector in driving smart city initiatives across Africa. Companies specialising in technology, telecommunications, and infrastructure partnered with governments and local authorities to implement smart solutions, such as IoT sensors, data analytics, and digital platforms, to improve urban living conditions. In recent years, innovation hubs and eco-systems have emerged in African cities, fostering entrepreneurship and innovation in the smart city space. These hubs serve as incubators for startups developing solutions tailored to local urban challenges, contributing to the growth of the smart city ecosystem in Africa. As smart cities continue to evolve in Africa, there is a growing emphasis on inclusivity and sustainability. Efforts are being made to ensure that smart solutions address the needs of all residents, including marginalised communities, and contribute to the long-term sustainability of urban development. Thus, the development of smart cities in Africa embodies a dynamic journey steered by technological advancements, governmental guidance, private-sector partnerships, and community involvement. With African cities undergoing rapid urbanisation, the significance of smart city endeavours is poised to grow substantially, shaping the trajectory of urban life across the continent.

Efforts such as Rwanda's Smart Cities Blueprint and various projects in cities like Niamey, Lagos, and Accra demonstrate Africa's advancement towards digital transformation and sustainable urban development. Unlike conventional Western models of smart cities, some African leaders advocate for a neo-vernacular approach, empowering communities to participate in the design and construction of their urban spaces. As Lovitske \[[37](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_37)\] asserted, initiatives such as HubCité in Lomé and Equo Atlantic in Lagos exemplify this approach, fostering locally driven urban projects and facilitating peer-to-peer exchanges to create more inclusive and culturally relevant smart cities. Smart cities in Africa, therefore, serve as symbols of progress and innovation. They spearhead technological advancements in urban areas by leveraging smart infrastructure, promoting data sharing, and fostering digital innovation to enhance the quality of life for residents and stimulate economic development across the continent. In Lagos, Nigeria, like in many other African cities, there is a surge in the number of young data scientists collaborating to establish new AI-driven companies and enhance their proficiency in machine learning techniques. Lagos State is said to hold a significant position within the Nigerian economy, serving as the nation's primary commercial hub and playing a pivotal role in the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS). With an estimated population exceeding 25 million, Lagos boasts an economy surpassing that of any other within the ECOWAS sub-region. Additionally, it serves as the epicentre of Nigeria's and West Africa's regional financial system, characterised by prominent banks, insurance companies, microfinance institutions, community banks, discount houses, and the capital market \[[38](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_38)\].

Furthermore, the story of Konza Technopolis in Kenya serves as an exemplary model for cities worldwide. Konza, a smart city developed from scratch, avoids inherited infrastructural and social challenges. The vision for Konza Technopolis is to become a world-class city driven by a thriving ICT sector, reliable infrastructure, and business-friendly governance systems. Development began in 2009, with plans to accommodate more than 185,000 residents and create 100,000 jobs by 2030. Konza prioritises inclusiveness by granting residents direct access to data collected within the city, enabling active participation in smart city operations and promoting sustainable living practices. Cape Town is recognised as one of Africa's smartest cities, offering an open data portal and promoting digital inclusion through free Wi-Fi on city buses. In other words, Cape Town stands out for its use of smart sensors and real-time data analysis. South Africa faces challenges in meeting demands for electricity, water, and waste management, prompting the adoption of IoT devices and real-time data collection. These sensors, deployed across major cities, gather data from various sources such as water and electricity meters, waste bins, and traffic lights. Network providers like Sqwidnet facilitate data sharing and enable municipalities to optimise various aspects, including farming, traffic management, crime detection, and waste collection.

## CHALLENGES AND SOLUTIONS

Smart cities across Africa are confronted with a myriad of challenges that seem to impede their developmental progress, thereby affecting smart citizens and Africa's economy at large. However, this chapter considers artificial intelligence (especially generative artificial intelligence) as a countermeasure tool in combatting hindrances to the sustainability of smart cities across Africa.

### Luddites and Factors Limiting the Sustainability of Smart Cities Across Africa

As argued by Mwakitalima, Rizan, and Kumar \[[39](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_39)\], Africa is experiencing a rapid population expansion in urban centres, and African leaders are showing high enthusiasm towards developing urban areas compared to rural regions, as these urban settings become focal points for social and economic activities. This trend is driven by factors such as rural-to-urban migration, particularly among the younger generation. Consequently, urban populations experience swift growth, resulting in the over exploitation of natural resources, environmental degradation, and heightened strain on social services. The rapid increase in population catalyses the construction of smart cities, aimed at meeting the needs of both current and future generations. To some scholars, high urbanisation, increased population growth, poor basic infrastructure, poverty, poor legislation and regulations, economic instability, and poor governance are some of the factors preventing the establishment of smart cities in Africa.

On sustainability challenges facing smart cities in Africa, starting with Northern Africa, significant issues include a high population density, low-income levels, youth unemployment, social exclusion, inadequate professional skills and education, and grappling with migration and refugee crises. Western Africa stands out as the most rapidly urbanising sub-region. Despite gaps in urban data reliability, the emergence of urban middle classes is deemed crucial for sustaining growth and attracting foreign direct investment. However, deficiencies persist in regional and local urban infrastructure, logistics, transportation, port facilities, Information and Communications Technologies (ICT), and energy resources. Additionally, climate change and political instability hinder sustainable development. Eastern Africa, while the least urbanised, experiences rapid urbanisation rates. It faces regional economic vulnerabilities with cities marked by high poverty and inequality levels. The region's heavy reliance on agriculture makes it vulnerable to climate change, exacerbating food insecurity and contributing to internal displacement and refugee inflows. Central Africa is also witnessing rapid urbanisation but is plagued by deep poverty, inequality, and unemployment. Climate change-induced threats such as food insecurity and deforestation, coupled with a corrupt political system, pose significant challenges. Municipalities struggle to generate revenues to fund essential services \[[40](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_40)\].

It is one thing to build a smart city but it is another thing to sustain the smart city and to further extend the smart initiatives to other parts of the state. People migrate to urban cities to enjoy the technological benefits therein and to better their lifestyles. This leads to the issue of overcrowding and a high rate of cybercrimes, amongst others. Imagine an African state with many cities, towns, and villages, but only a few cities are smart to the core. Imagine a state minister boosting drilling boreholes in his constituencies when in fact, in the 21st century, every house is supposed to have it. Imagine a state governor boosting or establishing some e-centres in the state, when in fact, the state as a whole is supposed to have been digitalised as everyone irrespective of location can have free access to the internet. What a colossal failure in the 21st century! Most African leaders are Luddites (little or no interest in technological change) and do not invest much in technological advancement, believing that it is too capital-expensive but forgetting that technology rules today's world, digitalising every aspect of human endeavour. This downplay of technological advancement is seen in either the state or nation's budget, as a small percentage of funds goes to technology. Concerning Nigeria's technological status:

> With a modest goal of capturing just 0.1% of the burgeoning industry's value over 5 years, Nigeria could potentially generate an annual industry worth $16 billion. This significant figure, solely derived from AI, underscores the tremendous potential of the sector. When considering the incorporation of Blockchain Technology, Fintech, IoT, Robotics, Clean Tech, Methanol, Hydrogen Production, and other innovations, the possibilities seem limitless. Nigeria, boasting a vast, young, intelligent, innovative, and adaptable workforce, along with an insatiable global demand for technology, stands poised to establish an alternative national wealth and economy that could surpass its current budget and economic scale.
> 
> \[[41](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_41)\]

Thus, a Luddite attitude towards technological advancement does not befit a 21st-century leader or head of state who has failed to prioritise the welfare of his people as technology has come to stay and is gradually becoming man's companion. A stretch of smart initiatives in an African state or nation is far better and more profitable than having a centralised one as residents or citizens would have equal access and benefits.

### African Smart Cities and Generative Artificial Intelligence as Enabler/Problem Solver

Technological advancements in smart cities are being crafted to tackle various challenges encompassing energy and water management, urban transportation, street lighting, and public safety. These breakthroughs are supported by overarching progress in fields like wireless communication, data analysis, and cloud computing \[[8](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_8)\].

There is no doubt that smart cities in Africa are facing sustainability challenges, amongst other things, but artificial intelligence (AI) has strengthened smart initiatives and projects in Africa. The same artificial intelligence, especially generative artificial intelligence (a subset of AI techniques and models that are designed to generate new data or content) can also serve as a problem-solving tool or technique in tackling the problems facing smart cities in Africa. Technology indeed has shaped smart cities through the following means: sensors (for monitoring public infrastructures, such as bridges, roads, and buildings, increase awareness by generating relevant data, which further enables the efficient use of resources), and big data (for enabling efficient resource management, traffic optimisation, and public safety initiatives). By analysing data from sensors and IoT devices deployed throughout the city, governments can make data-driven decisions to improve the quality of life for residents), AI (for empowering city officials with accurate tools to measure different city processes, make data-informed decisions faster, and enact changes that improve quality of life), geospatial technology and digital mapping (for supporting technologies such as 3D modelling and LiDAR, mobile mapping technologies, and remote sensing technologies). Tools also include photogrammetry, remote sensing, mapping, geographic information systems (GIS), geodesy, and others \[[6](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_6)\].

Leveraging smart technologies presents cities with an effective approach to addressing challenges arising from urban migration and issues related to service delivery, including inadequate housing, limited employment opportunities, and unreliable water supply \[[42](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_42)\]. As noted by McKinsey Global Institute \[[43](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_43)\], the strategic deployment of the existing generation of smart city applications has the potential to help cities achieve 70% of the United Nations’ Sustainable Development Goals. Smart solutions, such as smart mobility apps (including on-demand ride-sharing services, carpooling, car-hailing, bike rental, and bike-sharing applications), smart waste management systems, and smart grids, are transforming urban living. Smart waste management leverages IoT technology to provide real-time data on waste generation patterns and behaviours, enabling municipalities, cities, and waste collectors to optimize waste collection operations. Similarly, smart grids, equipped with IoT technology, enhance energy transmission efficiency and provide providers and distributors with better access to data on energy consumption. This empowers consumers to have more control over their individual energy decisions and smart air quality monitoring system. (Using IoT, a smart air quality monitoring solution can detect carbon dioxide levels, noxious gases, and pollutants, sending real-time data to a central management dashboard.) \[[6](#chapter8.xhtml#r8_6)\]

Generative AI, therefore, plays a vital role in dealing with the various challenges in smart cities across Africa by providing innovative solutions. AI algorithms can analyse vast amounts of data collected from sensors, social media, and other sources to identify patterns, trends, and anomalies. Generative AI can assist urban planners and architects in designing more efficient and sustainable cities. By generating and evaluating multiple design scenarios, AI algorithms can optimise land use, transportation networks, and infrastructure layouts to enhance liveability and reduce environmental impact. AI algorithms can optimise traffic flow, public transit routes, and ride-sharing services to reduce congestion and improve mobility. By analysing real-time data from vehicles and pedestrians, AI systems can dynamically adjust traffic signals, reroute buses, and allocate resources to minimise travel times and carbon emissions. AI can optimise energy production, distribution, and consumption in smart cities. By forecasting energy demand, identifying energy-saving opportunities, and optimising renewable energy integration, AI algorithms can help cities achieve their sustainability goals and reduce reliance on fossil fuels. Generative AI can optimise waste collection routes, recycling programs, and landfill usage to minimise environmental impact and improve efficiency. Generative AI algorithms can analyse video feeds, sensor data, and social media posts to detect and respond to security threats and emergencies in real time. Generative AI can facilitate citizen engagement and participation in smart city initiatives. Generative AI can help cities prepare for and respond to natural disasters such as floods, hurricanes, and wildfires. By simulating disaster scenarios, predicting evacuation routes, and coordinating emergency response efforts, AI systems can improve resilience and mitigate the impact of catastrophic events on urban infrastructure and communities.

## CONCLUSION

Smart initiatives and projects in urban cities attract people of diverse cultures, status, gender, and religion. The realisation of smart cities across Africa has mitigated some of the challenges facing African nations, and the impact of the few smart cities is heavily felt all over the African continent as Africans are benefiting from them. Nevertheless, more needs to be done to digitalise every place in Africa to prevent problems such as overcrowding from escalating and other serious problems such as cybercrimes. To this effect, sustainability guarantees the future of smart cities across Africa, and with technological tools (such as generative AI) and technophile attitude/mindset (investing in new technological ideas and projects), creating more smart cities across Africa as well as sustaining them can be guaranteed.

## RECOMMENDATIONS

From the preceding findings, the following recommendations were made to ensure sustainable and resilient smart cities across Africa:

1.  African leaders should be technophiles to the core not to be left behind in the 21st-century technological innovations.
    
2.  Ethical protocols and regulations must be established to govern the responsible utilisation of generative AI in Africa, addressing issues such as privacy, consent, and the potential impact of AI-generated content. Africans should start thinking for themselves to enrich themselves with technical/pragmatic knowledge.
    
3.  The African government should spare no fund in getting the best out of technological advancement to secure the future of smart cities across Africa.

# 9Smart City Governance and Citizen Engagement

Sunanda Vincent Jaiwant, Dheepa T., and Ajitha Haridasan

DOI: [10.1201/9781003497189-9](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003497189-9)

## INTRODUCTION

In an era of swift urbanisation and technological progress, towns all over the world are becoming what are widely referred to as “smart cities” (SC). The objective of these urban landscapes is to improve the standard of living for their inhabitants through the incorporation of state-of-the-art technologies, data-driven decision-making, and an emphasis on sustainability. The complex interplay between smart city administration and citizen engagement—two pillars that influence the fundamental nature of urban life in the twenty-first century—lays the groundwork for this paradigm shift.

Urban infrastructure is under increasing strain as a result of the enormous rural-to-urban migration that has occurred in the twenty-first century. The idea of “smart cities” has come to light as a hopeful response to these difficulties, offering effective and long-lasting solutions to the problems associated with urban complexity. Smart city efforts aim to optimise resource allocation, improve infrastructure, and enhance overall urban functionality by utilising technologies like artificial intelligence (AI), data analytics, and the Internet of Things (IoT).

Nevertheless, the incorporation of technical solutions is not the only factor that determines the success of smart city initiatives. These initiatives’ efficacy is heavily impacted by the governance frameworks that support them. Thus, the management of smart cities becomes essential to coordinating the smooth incorporation of technology advancements into urban landscape. Moreover, the concept of citizen engagement presents itself as a crucial element in actualising the goals of smart city development. Cities are realising the importance of incorporating citizens in decision-making processes, which is reshaping traditional paradigms of governance. In smart cities, citizen engagement goes beyond simple involvement in local issues and includes active cooperation, information sharing, and co-creation of urban areas ([Angelidou, M., 2014](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_3)).

This study aims to investigate and evaluate the complex relationships between citizen participation and smart city administration. The goal of this study is to provide a nuanced knowledge of how governance structures influence and are influenced by citizen participation in the setting of smart cities by examining case studies, exploring the body of current literature, and developing a thorough framework. This study aims to add to the expanding body of knowledge about the mutually beneficial link between smart city governance and citizen involvement by examining effective models, difficulties encountered, and opportunities for future advancements. The chapter also intends to analyse the relevant research works to examine the trends in smart city governance and AI. This study will also provide insights into emerging issues and challenges.

## LITERATURE REVIEW

### Smart City—Definition and Characteristics

The idea of a “smart city” emerged from urbanisation, which is characterised by a city's constant population growth. The growing urban population places a great demand on amenities, social sustainability, and living standards; in reality, it is difficult for the limited resources and services now in place to meet the demands of the populace. As a result, the smart city concept is developed as a means of integrating people, processes, data, and computing technology in a strategic manner. Big Data, cloud computing, and the Internet of Things are examples of technologies that can be connected with physical infrastructure to enable resourceful and efficient services and provide a sustainable level of living for the populace. The process of converting a city into a smart city is intricate and multifaceted, and it adapts quickly to the requirements of its citizens ([Vasudavan, H. Gunasekaran, S. S., & Balakrishnan, S., 2019](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_29)). In the twenty-first century, smart cities are a quickly developing notion that will change urban growth. Smart cities enhance the standard of living for their residents, boost the effectiveness of services and infrastructure, and encourage long-term, sustainable economic growth through the use of cutting-edge technologies and data analytics. Transportation, energy, health, education, and government are just a few of the disciplines that smart cities combine to create an intelligent and connected metropolitan environment ([Gracias, J. S., Parnell, G. S., Specking, E., Pohl, E. A., & Buchanan, R., 2023a](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_13), [2023b](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_12)). Globally, the idea of a “smart city” is becoming more and more popular as a solution to urbanisation, environmental issues, and economic expansion ([Yin, C., Xiong, Z., Chen, H., Wang, J., Cooper, D., & David, B., 2015a](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_33), [2015b](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_34)). The idea of smart cities is becoming more and more relevant as technology becomes more and more integrated into our everyday lives. Governments and the corporate sector have made significant investments in smart city initiatives in recent years. As technology develops and urban populations rise, smart cities are becoming more and more significant ([Ismagilova, E., Hughes, L., Dwivedi, Y. K., & Raman, K. R., 2019a](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_16), [2019b](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_17)). There is an increasing demand for rigorous study on the state of smart city research and applications because of the large global investments made in these initiatives by governments and the commercial sector. In recent years, there has been a great deal of interest in and investment in smart cities, with several initiatives and projects being carried out around the globe. Nonetheless, there isn’t a single, accepted definition of what constitutes a smart city, despite all of this interest and investment ([Tahmasseby, S., 2022](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_27)). For those involved in developing and implementing smart city programs, including legislators, city planners, and stakeholders, the absence of a precise definition may present difficulties.

### Governance in Smart Cities

Applications of smart city governance have the power to completely change how cities function by fostering greater accountability, openness, and involvement from the public. Smart cities can optimise the use of urban infrastructure and resources by gathering and analysing vast volumes of data in real time through the use of Internet of Things (IoT) devices and sensors. For example, by utilising sensors to monitor traffic flow and modify traffic lights in real time, smart city technology can reduce congestion and improve road safety ([Shoaib, N.; Shamsi, J. A., 2019](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_25)). Applications for smart cities can also give drivers real-time information on traffic conditions by analysing data from GPS devices and traffic sensors. These applications can then recommend alternate routes and ways to go around gridlock to help cars avoid it ([Din, I. U., Guizani, M., Rodrigues, J. J. P. C., Hassan, S., & Korotaev, V. V., 2019](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_8)). By opening up new avenues for interaction and communication between the populace and their government, smart city technology can boost citizen participation in addition to enhancing the efficacy and efficiency of public services. As a result, the general quality of life in the city may be raised and citizen-government cooperation may be strengthened ([Li, X., Liu, H., Wang, W., Zheng, Y., Lv, H., & Lv, Z., 2021](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_22)). The management and regulation of urban affairs by public organisations needs to change to accommodate the expanded scope of governance in smart cities, which goes beyond conventional frameworks. It entails the coordination of intricate networks of participants, including citizens, businesses, and government organisations. A proactive attitude towards technological improvements, agility, and adaptability are hallmarks of effective smart city governance. The governing structures of smart cities are crucial in determining the course and efficacy of technology initiatives. The historical hierarchical models are being redesigned to keep up with the speed at which innovation is occurring. Agile governance frameworks, decentralised decision-making, and cooperative methods are becoming crucial for negotiating the complexity of the technology frontier ([Yigitcanlar, T., Kamruzzaman, M., Foth, M., Sabatini-Marques, J., Da-Costa, E., & Ioppolo, G., 2019](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_31)).

### Citizen Engagement

Incorporating citizens into decision-making processes is an essential component of governance in smart cities. The smart city paradigm sees citizens as active players in influencing their urban environment, in contrast to the traditional role of citizens as beneficiaries of government services. Smart city citizen engagement models encompass a variety of approaches, such as crowdsourcing and participatory platforms, as well as co-creation projects that involve residents working with government agencies to design and execute solutions. Citizens in smart cities become into environmentally conscious individuals. Additionally, smart cities are more responsible settings for public initiatives like IT programs or projects involving involved residents. One of the most pressing demands in the globe right now is energy conservation, and the SC authorities have a great opportunity to create substantial savings with such a prudent approach. In view of the current energy crisis, such initiatives can help households save money on energy and have a genuine economic impact ([Jasińska-Biliczak, A., 2022](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_18)). As citizen participation is the key component of smart cities, it is imperative that local governments give priority to projects that let citizens participate in decision-making. Involving the public in the design and execution of smart city projects may guarantee that everyone's voice is heard and that the projects are adapted to the needs of the community. Hosting community forums and gatherings is one method of encouraging citizen participation in smart cities. Residents can voice their complaints and thoughts during these gatherings, and they can also talk about possible solutions to local problems. Building confidence between citizens and local authorities is essential for any smart city project to succeed, and community gatherings can help with this. Surveys and feedback systems are additional tools for including the public in smart city projects. These resources can offer insightful information on citizens’ goals and wants, enabling communities to better target their activities towards the requirements of the local population. In order to enable cities to make data-driven decisions about upcoming projects, surveys can also be used to assess the success of smart city efforts ([Cohem, B., n.d](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_6).).

Another effective instrument for citizen participation in smart cities is social media. Cities may communicate with inhabitants in both directions and reach a larger audience by utilising social media channels. Social media can be utilised to solicit resident input and ideas as well as to disseminate information about impending smart city initiatives. Involving citizens is essential to the success of smart city projects. Participating locals in the design and execution of these projects can guarantee that everyone's voice is heard and that the projects are suited to the needs of the community. Social media, questionnaires, feedback channels, and community gatherings are all effective strategies for encouraging citizen participation in smart cities. Cities can create more liveable and sustainable communities that benefit all people by placing a high priority on public engagement ([Gracias, J. S., Parnell, G. S., Specking, E., Pohl, E. A., & Buchanan, R., 2023a](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_13), [2023b](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_12)).

Through information and communication technology (ICT), primarily the internet, e-participation enables citizens to actively engage in the public consultation process. This idea refers to the ways in which ICT enables interactions between official political and administrative authorities and the general public. By using contemporary technologies to promote active social participation in the decision-making process, e-participation allows the government to reach a larger number of citizens than traditional social participation methods ([Sobaci, Z., 2010](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_26)). There are many different e-participation methods available, such as web apps, chat rooms, discussion forums, videoconferencing, social media (Facebook, X), e-petitions, online questionnaires, geo-questionnaires, and geo-discussions. These instruments are all dependent on ICT. One widely held belief is that intelligent ICTs can enhance citizens’ quality of life ([Bolívar, M. P., 2016](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_5)). At the leading edge of the smart city revolution, information and communication technology (ICT) is causing radical shifts in urban living. The strategic integration of ICT in smart cities is a fundamental shift that goes beyond simple technological advancement to improve the lives of city dwellers ([Dameri, R. P., & Cocchia, A., 2013](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_7)). According to [Yigitcanlar and Lee (Yigitcanlar, T., & Lee, S. H., 2014)](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_32), smart ICTs are embedded in objects, wireless, and use sensors to record their surroundings. In sectors like healthcare, real estate, utilities, transportation, public safety, and administration, they offer the vital infrastructure for more intelligent and networked solutions ([Washburn, D., Sindhu, U., Balaouras, S., Dines, R. A., Hayes, N., & Nelson, L. E., 2009](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_30)). Smart ICTs, for instance, assist in gathering and disseminating consumption data to optimise energy management in the energy grid sector ([Farmanbar, M., Parham, K., Arild, Ø., & Rong, C., 2019](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_9))

When it comes to transportation, smart ICTs make it possible for multimodal networks to be secure, inclusive of all social groups, and sustainable. This makes it easier for citizens to commute. ICT improves public safety by enabling predictive policing, emergency response systems, and smart surveillance. A safer urban environment is facilitated by sensor technology, emergency mobile apps, and integrated camera networks. Rapid emergency response times, enhanced situational awareness, and general security measures that are better all benefit citizens. By guaranteeing accessible for all individuals, including those with mobility problems, smart transportation systems encourage social inclusion. Real-time information about parking availability, accessible routes, and public transportation timetables is provided by ICT-enabled platforms. With the help of mobile applications, people with a variety of needs can plan and carry out their travels more easily. Smart ICTs make urban mobility more egalitarian and inclusive by lowering obstacles to transit ([Herrenkind, B., Nastjuk, I., Brendel, A. B., Trang, S., & Kolbe, L. M., 2019](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_15)). Smart ICTs have a major positive impact on the security of urban transportation systems. Intelligent traffic management systems monitor and optimise traffic flow by using real-time data from cameras, sensors, and linked vehicles. Road safety is increased, when possible, and dangers are identified and addressed by automated systems, which lowers the likelihood of accidents. Smart ICTs also make it possible for linked car technologies to be developed, which improves safety precautions by enabling automobiles to communicate with infrastructure and one another ([Nikitas, A., Kougias, I., Alyavina, E., & Njoya Tchouamou, E., 2017](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_24)). By dramatically lowering the energy demand over the course of a residential or commercial building's lifetime, smart ICTs can contribute to the establishment of so-called “zero energy buildings” in the building industry ([Kylili, A., & Fokaides, P. A., 2015](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_21)). Smart wearables can be used in the healthcare industry to effectively monitor public health ([Trang, S., Trenz, M., Weiger, W., Tarafdar, M., & Cheung, C., 2020](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_28)). Smart wearables are now essential parts of remote healthcare delivery, going beyond their use as fitness trackers. These gadgets enable remote medical condition diagnosis, medication prescription, and treatment administration by healthcare professionals thanks to their sophisticated sensors and networking characteristics. Smart wearables have a revolutionary effect on improving accessibility to medical treatment, removing geographical boundaries, and delivering healthcare services directly to individuals. Advanced sensors found in smart wearables, like temperature, pulse oximeter, and electrocardiogram (ECG) sensors, allow for remote monitoring and diagnosis of a range of medical issues. By enabling healthcare practitioners to collect data in real time, these gadgets eliminate the need for in-person visits to medical institutions and enable the early detection of anomalies and prompt diagnosis. Smart wearables provide remote diagnosis, medication prescriptions, and patient treatment ([Ghazal, T. M., Hasan, M. K., Alshurideh, M. T., Alzoubi, H. M., Ahmad, M., Akbar, S. S., Al Kurdi, B., & Akour, I. A., 2021](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_11)). A revolution in education has been brought about by smart ICTs, which have transformed the traditional classroom paradigm into a dynamic and adaptable learning environment. With the help of intelligent ICTs, students can participate in interactive learning experiences and can study whenever, wherever, and at their own pace. Smart ICTs in the education sector facilitate more interactive learning where students can “learn at anytime, anywhere, in any way, and at any pace.” Although smart information and communication technologies (ICTs) are revolutionising many different fields, there is no one-size-fits-all use for them. In certain domains, including education or public administration, real-time data processing might not be the primary focus. On the other hand, smart ICTs are critical enablers in fields like energy or transportation management, necessitating powerful big data processing skills. Smart ICTs play a more limited role in fields like education or public administration, where processing large volumes of data in real time is typically not required. In contrast, in domains like energy or transportation management, smart ICTs are essential enablers and require big data processing capabilities ([Neirotti, P., De Marco, A., Cagliano, A. C., Mangano, G., & Scorrano, F., 2014](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_23)).

## SMART GOVERNANCE THROUGH ICT IN SMART CITIES

The idea of smart cities has garnered a lot of attention from researchers and policymakers during the past 20 years. The concept of a “smart city” is not well defined, even though it has been discussed extensively in the literature ([Albino, V., Berardi, U., & Dangelico, R. M., 2015](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_2); [Angelidou, M., 2014](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_3); [Anthopoulos, L., 2015](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_4)). It typically refers to settings where individuals are provided with cutting-edge services through the use of information and communication technologies (ICTs) to improve their well-being and promote sustainable economic growth ([Yigitcanlar, T. Kamruzzaman, M., Foth, M., Sabatini-Marques, J., Da-Costa, E., & Ioppolo, G., 2019](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_31)). Smart cities are characterised by their smart economy, smart people, smart governance, smart mobility, smart environment, and smart living ([Giffinger, R., Fertner, C., Kramar, H., Kalasek, R., Pichler-Milanovic, N., & Meijers, E., 2007](#chapter9.xhtml#r9_10)). These attributes address important issues like economic competitiveness, citizen education, social interaction quality, labour market flexibility, governmental strategies, innovative transportation systems, sustainable resource management, and public safety.

### Smart City Governance Models

#### Central governance systems

The participation of citizens in smart cities is influenced top-down by centralised governing institutions. This paradigm allows for the effective execution of city-wide projects by consolidating decision-making authority in the hands of local officials. Although this strategy can hasten the implementation of smart technologies, it might make citizen engagement more difficult. Citizens’ influence is frequently restricted to offering suggestions for already-formulated programs. The effectiveness of public engagement depends on how quickly centralised organisations respond to feedback obtained via online social networks and other means. Finding a balance between effectiveness and inclusivity is the difficult part. Within the context of smart city implementation, centralised governance models are essential for coordinating all-encompassing plans. Online social networks can be effectively used to distribute information, gather input, and promote citizen engagement when they are integrated into centralised frameworks. Centralised platforms overseen by local government officials have the potential to optimise communication routes and offer a cohesive interface for residents to obtain details about ongoing projects, voice concerns, and actively engage in the decision-making process.

#### Decentralised governance systems

Online social networks are a common tool used by decentralised governance models in smart cities to foster a more open and interactive atmosphere. In this instance, citizens participate in the urban narrative in addition to being information consumers. Decentralised platforms facilitate the natural development of grassroots movements, community-led projects, and cooperative problem-solving. Online social networks allow residents to interact, exchange ideas, and work together to address urban concerns. They act as catalysts for decentralised engagement.

#### Hybrid systems

The balance between decentralised involvement and centralised control is achieved by hybrid governance frameworks. With this strategy, residents, businesses, and government agencies are all connected through online social networks, which serve as bridges in a cooperative ecosystem. These technologies provide prompt communication, guaranteeing individuals stay informed and contributing significantly to decision-making procedures. Hybrid approaches create a dynamic framework for smart city administration by acknowledging the significance of both bottom-up innovation and top-down direction.

## GOVERNANCE AND CITIZEN PARTICIPATION

As smart cities gain traction, governance plays an increasingly important role in coordinating technology integration and encouraging public participation. This study examines the complex relationship between citizen involvement and governance in smart cities, focusing on how citizen participation affects governance dynamics and how governance structures affect the use of ICTs. The emergence of smart cities signals a paradigm change in urban governance, as residents play an active role in determining their cities’ destiny rather than just being users of services. At the centre of this change is citizen engagement, which uses ICTs to build an urban ecology that is more inclusive and collaborative. Citizen engagement has historically taken several forms, such as public consultations and town hall gatherings in [Figures 9.1](#chapter9.xhtml#f9_1) and [9.2](#chapter9.xhtml#f9_2). These conventional approaches are enhanced by digital platforms in the smart city era, allowing for dynamic involvement, feedback loops, and real-time interactions. The development of citizen participation is indicative of a dedication to openness, responsiveness, and collaborative decision-making.
