# 17Moving Beyond GenderRecognizing Equality and Inclusivity Across Cultures Through Feminist AI

Hitanshi Goel

DOI: [10.1201/9781003497189-17](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003497189-17)

## INTRODUCTION

Artificial intelligence (AI) technologies have advanced expeditiously, enabling computers and machines to carry out an extent of activities imitating human cognitive functions from perception to decision-making \[[1](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_1)\]. Nowadays, the AI systems undeniably have become an indispensable part of operating systems of our lives in the form of search engines, medical diagnostics, and surveillance technologies. However, there is need to divert our attention to the phenomenal intersection of AI and ‘epistemic injustice’ which underscores the potential of AI to perpetuate bias and ingrained power dynamics that allow for their gendered discrimination. Epistemic injustice refers to the practices that undermine individuals fairly as knowers or even distort their understanding within the recognized institutions and practices \[[2](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_2)\]. Such interplays of AI with epistemic injustice have to be realized and attended to in a manner that AI technologies do not further magnify the present biases and injustices but assist in favouring justice and fairness, an inclusive representation of diverse voices.

Though the emergence of AI poses multifaceted consideration, it also provides wide opportunities for the well-being of many, and contributing to the achievement of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) \[[3](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_3)\]. At the same time, it carries intrinsic risks towards boosting differences in economic, cognitive, gender, and cultural domains. Nevertheless, it promises enhanced efficiency and productivity despite the ethical concerns surrounding the deployment of AI and apart from the fact that, being able to mix and exacerbate prejudices, it may help to destroy the environment and open new gaps, compared to the current ones, attacking human dignity by reinforcing prejudices, discrimination, and victimization based on stereotypes \[[4](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_4)\]. Recognizing the imperative of inclusive decision-making, global frameworks such as the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development Goals underline that women and diverse communities have the right to their participation in all aspects of decision-making processes. However, despite incremental strides, these cohorts remain predominately marginalized within the AI domain in the literal sense of lacking ‘active involvement as decision-makers’ and this happens even though many ethical questions are raised about the AI system that are bound to have an effect in pivotal areas like governance, social interaction, democracy, legal frameworks, environmental sustainability, data privacy, gender equality, and protection of human rights. The full potential of these technologies can only be realized if these concerns are considered during their development and deployment, based on principles of inclusivity, equity, and ethical integrity. In a world which is evolving rapidly around AI technologies, there is a need to have ethical frameworks and governance that reflect diversity, equity, and inclusion, and thus contribute towards the comprehensive attainment of social justice \[[5](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_5)\]. AI systems pose new ethical challenges that go beyond its impact on decision-making processes and extend to diverse areas, such as employment dynamics, labour relations, social interactions, provision of healthcare, paradigms of education, media landscapes, access to information, mitigation of the digital divide, preservation of personal data and consumer rights, environmental sustainability, democratic governance principles, due recourse to rule of law, and improvement in security and policing strategies \[[6](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_6)\]. There is a necessity to include due diligence, even on the part of the technologies themselves, with particular attention to dual-use technologies, and on human rights and fundamental freedoms, such as the right to freedom of expression, privacy, and non-discrimination \[[7](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_7)\].

AI algorithms have the ability to replicate, deepen, and bolster pre-existing biases in all their profound forms of prejudice, discrimination, and stereotyping. This ability of AI to replicate and fortify the biases is the due to the fact that AI can carry out tasks formerly conceived to be carried out by living beings and often thought to be exclusively a human prerogative. This new capability enables AI to produce and influence human practices, societal structures, and our interrelation with the environment and ecosystems in an unparalleled manner \[[8](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_8)\]. Critically, AI is the context within which children and adolescents develop their understanding of the world, form identity, evaluate media critically, build information critically, and develop skills in making decisions. In due course, AI might even shrink the gap between human experience and the power of independent endeavour, thereby raising even more concerns over human self-consciousness, the aptitude for interaction within social, cultural, and environmental contexts, autonomous and self-governed intrinsic worth, and inherent dignity \[[9](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_9)\].

In this regard, a feminist approach to AI governance can allow a hospitable environment for gender equality and diversity inclusion issues at every stage of the AI cycle, right from its conception to its deployment. A feminist lens of AI would, therefore, assist in understanding how an AI system could exacerbate or even normalize the presence of gender-based violence (GBV) and gender-based inequities (GBI) by centring the experiences, perspectives, and priorities of the marginalized genders \[[10](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_10)\]. This approach may present some strategies that would assure proactive embedding effects of inclusivity, representation, and equity in the design, development, deployment, and monitoring of AI technologies. Feminist AI governance is a new domain of policy, research, advocacy, and development that aims to ensure the governance, design, development, deployment, and use of AI systems should be carried out in just, fair, and inclusive ways. Therefore, feminist AI governance seeks to respond to the power imbalance in the AI ecosystem with an approach that seeks to redress this through a regime guided by structural inequalities, colonial legacies, and multi-layered harms to society \[[11](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_11)\]. Grounded in feminist values and a critical conception of technology in society, feminist AI governance reconceptualizes that algorithm is not merely a mathematical entity, but a contextual artefact conditioned by social relations and cultural values \[[12](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_12)\]. Feminist AI governance would ensure that the gendered social, political, and historical context and all other intersectional considerations are interrogated in the development and deployment process of AI systems for harm prevention and to deliver equity. Algorithm assemblage is not to be conceived as a simple sum of heterogenous technologies, but as a configuration that seems to blend mathematical and infrastructural ingredients with social, political, and cultural factors. So, with a much more conceptual identification of the algorithms as sociotechnical assemblages, much beyond their characteristic as abstract entities, it points at the potential ways by which they may reproduce inequality and hold deep societal implications. Algorithmic systems, in other words, do not exist in a vacuum but are rather infiltrated and shaped by the values, assumptions, and biases that appear in their development and deployment. Such systems may serve to reinforce and intensify existing power relations, marginalization, and structural injustices. Thus, any critical review of the social, political, and historical contexts that shape algorithmic assemblages should be more committed to reduction of harm and pursuit of equity.

Feminist AI governance subjects a critical look at the system as a product of power relationships, which is inherently responsive in relation to the economic, historical, socio-political, legal, and cultural context from which it arises and is deployed, so that it exposes their nature as historically and situationally contingent \[[13](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_13)\]. The systemic or structural approach acknowledges ‘harm’ to be multidimensional and targets both, the marginalized bodies and territories of the global majority. Starting with the commitment to achieve integral and planetary justice, feminist AI governance broadens our view of the AI system to encompass the whole life cycle of an AI system, rather than limiting itself to simply AI or data pipeline. The experiences, perspectives, and priorities of the most adversely affected by AI systems are centred in order to highlight the modalities through which AI technologies are likely to produce and entrench new forms of oppression, discrimination, and inequality. It surfaces the organizational and technical level strategies that identify ways to embed the principles of inclusivity, representation, and equity in the design, development, deployment, and monitoring of AI. Against such a backdrop of AI, which is understood as a neutral or objective set of tools, feminist AI governance insists on at least paying equal attention to the social, political, and historical horizons within which algorithmic assemblages arise. Systems do not emerge in a vacuum but, in fact, are loaded with values, assumption, and bias that percolate power structures and sociotechnical environments in which they are developed. These contexts need to be critically appraised in the aim of mitigating, if not eliminating, harm that challenges present inequalities and fosters real fairness in how AI technologies are thought of and used.

While redressing the inherent deeply rooted imbalances exclusion in AI ecosystems with a long history and deep-rooted inequities which acted as the major source of both marginalization and exclusion, they must be systematically based on a human rights model. These disturbing realities need to be engaged and dealt across multiple layers such as at individual, communal, institutional, and systemic levels. This will, therefore, substantially help to not only foster real inclusion but also accelerate sustainable development and build peaceful societies, all in alignment with the motives of the 2030 Agenda for SDGs. It should necessarily foreground justice, human rights, and equality at every phase of the conceptualization, development, deployment, and utilization of AI systems to enhance their efficacy, infuse confidence in the operation, and ensure that the resultantly beneficially and adversely implicated persons, and particularly so for those belonging to marginalized groups, can affirm their fundamental entitlement to participate in shaping the trajectory of our societies. This underscores the imperative of ensuring equitable access to technological advancements.

A human rights-based framework ‘sheds light on’ ways to infuse the principles of equity, inclusion, and substantive representation throughout the AI life cycle proactively. It calls for a critical reflection on how these AI systems may be reinforced towards already existing forms of oppression, discrimination, and marginalization – more often than not – against global majority communities that have traditionally been burdened with more than their share of such ills. This approach further views harm as a multidimensional outcome that is never purely technical or economic. These observances should pay attention to the multifaceted effects that AI systems could bring in various human domains such as social, cultural, environmental, political, and so on.

## BIAS IN AI SYSTEMS

Use of AI presents key opportunities to enhance well-being in all categories of people noted above and to achieve the SDGs. However, on the other hand, it may boost discrepancies in the field of economy, knowledge, and influence between genders and cultures. While AI has the capacity to enhance effectiveness and output, it also brings forth significant ethical dilemmas such as being prone to the reinforcement of biases; contribution towards environmental degradation; worsening pre-existing inequalities; marginalization of the vulnerable communities; and ultimately undervaluing human dignity through the vehicle of bias, discrimination, and stereotyping \[[14](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_14)\]. The AI technologies open up a huge number of opportunities for benefiting people and their nations, while at the same time, they give an essential rise to important ethical dilemmas ranging from the biases that may be perpetuated and, in turn, could also lead even to prejudices and associated injustices, imbalances in digital access, exclusion, challenges of cultural, social, and biological diversity, economic, or social divisions \[[15](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_15)\]. Other concerns focus on the necessity for transparency and clarity in the functioning of algorithms and the data used in their training, along with their outcomes in terms of respect to human dignity, human rights, gender equality, democracy, socio-economic dynamics, and political and cultural mechanisms. Currently, AI systems are designed, developed, managed, and assessed, without systematic consideration towards diversity and gender balance. Further, according to the 2020 report of GPAI Responsible AI Working Group and The Future Society, even AI initiatives self-identifying as ‘AI for SDG’ or ‘ethical AI’ demonstrate a gap in due involvement of the Global South and marginalized communities, such as persons with disabilities, indigenous groups, the LGBTI+ community, people living under the poverty line, and migrants \[[16](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_16)\].

The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development Goals, among other global governance frameworks, is seen as a very solid and coherent tool to contribute to the entitlement of women and diverse communities to take part in all decision-making processes \[[17](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_17)\]. However, despite these incremental developments, the mentioned communities stay relatively excluded from AI practice and the decision-making process, even when the ethical considerations of AI systems touch upon some of the vital domains such as governance, societal interactions, democratic processes, legal frameworks, environmental preservation, and data security. The consequences of marginalization of the underrepresented community on AI technology advancement and adoption are damaging as that would mean that AI is not able to fully realize its potential for advancing the society \[[18](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_18)\]. AI technologies possess the capacity to exacerbate prevailing disparities and inequities within and between nations, thus, it is imperative to ensure that principles of justice, confidence, and impartiality are sustained, such that no nation or individual is left out in view of questions of equitable access to AI technologies and their benefits or protection from the adverse effects that might rise. A human rights perspective placed at the centre of AI governance, with a focus on the perspective and voices of the marginalized groups, can help formulate strategies for systematic integration of principles of inclusion, representation, and justice at each and every stage of the AI life cycle. This will thus stimulate the participatory ability of the marginalized communities to shape their priorities, be of concern in the evolution and governance of AI systems. It is important to understand the extensive implications that AI technologies have on various aspects of human existence, particularly for those communities of the global majority that have historically borne the weight of systemic oppression and marginalization. This has resulted in perpetuating the discrepancy not only in the voices but also obstructs the potential of AI to encourage significant advancements towards the realization of SDGs.

Bias in AI systems usually emanates as an unintentional by-product of the biases in society and real-world data not being adequately employed in training the AI system. It is an inborn prejudice in AI models, precisely in ‘Deep Neural Networks’ (DNNs), since the AI models are just a mathematical representation modelled on the human brain \[[19](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_19)\]. DNNs inherently have patterns and associations just like humans since they have a lot of layering of neurons, modelled from human or biological neurons. A newly created neural network is just like a ‘new born child’ that needs to be trained on real-world data so that the network can learn and grow into its ability \[[19](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_19)\]. As a matter of fact, training at this stage is found to be one of those stages where biases are introduced inadvertently and perpetuated. For example, most of the images portrayed with the word ‘nurse’ will be feminine, and in the case of ‘CEO,’ those will be masculine, hence ending up reinforcing gender stereotypes \[[20](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_20)\]. The design of AI itself is not solely responsible for these biases. It is a consequence of combining the biases living within the data used for model training, not only in terms of the lack of variation or representativeness but all human decisions that were made from data labelling to feature selection, as well as model architectures. These human-influenced decisions can ironically sustain and support biases and blind spots. AI systems are not designed to be partial, but they learn and optimize based on biased input data presented to them by humans and development frameworks. As a result, it reinforces such patterns and associations. This will require proactively finding diverse, representative training data, coupled with critical human bias reviews and remedies, from the design phase through to the development life cycle of the AI and only through such deliberative and proactive processes realization of an unbiased AI can be made possible.

The sense of inclusion goes much further than the integration of what is considered traditionally marginalized groups, it implies breaking down multifaceted forms of discrimination that add to continued isolation or marginalization and are based on arbitrary elements such as age, gender, or skin colour. This, therefore, requires a transition from just treatment to proactive measures in diversity and gender parity, encompassing resource allocation, oversight, and disclosure. This change also means challenging age old power dynamics, including power and resource allocation. Bias in AI can, therefore, occur at any step of its development, from its inception and choice of modelling to data acquisition and processing, up to the context of its application. These biases typically fall into three primary categories: biases related to data, algorithm selection, and deployment \[[21](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_21)\]. Some of these data biases include measurement biases, which occur in the selection or collection of some features. For example, with predictive analytics of AI, based on height in relation to age might fail to include variations across different genders or ethnicity, a selection bias is witnessed, hence a wrong prediction. Representation bias arises if the training data doesn’t adequately represent many of the demographic groups, thus limiting the possibility to derive generalizations from it. In order to overcome this bias, there is a need to collect more data from such under-represented groups which might be a challenge due to privacy concerns. Algorithm selection biases include aggregation bias, where a uniform model fails to accommodate data diversity, such as binary gender models failing to accommodate non-binary identities. Learning bias also occurs in the selection of the model or learning approach when it increases disparities like an AI system disregarding data depending on perceived completeness or validity, and favouring some kind of input since the beginning. Deployment biases include circumstances whereby the AI systems find their way into different contexts from the environment of their development and hence give inappropriate outcomes. Last is the post-deployment feedback bias, which is realized when the process of adjusting models, informed by user input, does not consider demographic diversity and hence leads to new biases.

In today's world, Large Language Models (LLMs) have become public and are being employed in many walks of life by people from around the world, sharing information, explaining ideas, or even helping with some kind of cognitive task. There may be particular challenges posed by their unique structure and applications given the huge scale and complexity they present, combined with the common reuse and repurposing, diverse range of contexts to which they are put, including intricate developmental trajectories. The sheer scale, adaptability, and complexity of developmental paths for LLMs present a huge challenge in handling bias and preventing harm at both individual and societal levels \[[22](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_22)\]. The intrinsic bias in LLMs, notably in the areas of gender and geographical representation, offers yet more examples of the sheer scale of the challenge that has to be met. Some of the risks associated are erosion of social cohesion, GBV, and the marginalization of disadvantaged communities. To deal with these demands, approaches that include legal, social, or moral solutions besides technological ones are required in order to assure that AI deployment will be fair and responsible. Tackling the inherent inequalities in the AI ecosystems requires a systematic approach firmly based on principles of human rights and social justice which is also in furtherance of the SDGs agenda of 2030.

## HUMAN RIGHTS BASED AND ETHICAL AI

The use of AI technologies offers a range of possibilities in the direction of maximizing human welfare and at the same time opens doors for the nations to gain enormous benefits, but this breakthrough comes hand in hand with profound ethical challenges and call for their careful consideration and tackling \[[23](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_23)\]. The most relevant issue to follow is the fact that AI systems tend to integrate and worsen biases, which can ultimately result in discrimination, inequalities, digital divides, exclusionism while threatening the cultural, social, and biological diversity and the economic disparities. In addition, there is a need for transparency and clear insight on how the algorithms work, including the data used for training. AI systems may affect several core aspects of human life, such as human dignity, human rights, gender equality, democracy, socio-economic behaviour, political structures, cultural traditions, scientific endeavours, environmental protection, animal rights, and ecological systems. All these effects of AI on society, environment, ecosystem and even on human welfare, including psychological dimensions, are huge and continuously developing, needing a comprehensive examination. Given the current innovative ways in which AI is influencing human cognition, the patterns by which humans are interacting, making decisions, educational techniques, and their influence on scientific exploration, creative cultural production, and even information dissemination are particularly important \[[24](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_24)\].

Proper utilization of the AI systems will depend on transparency and explicitness in explaining AI capabilities and its limits so that people know when and to what extent AI is included in making the decisions. This aspect is particularly relevant as it will ensure that these are adapted in the right context, such as in cases of potential conflicts with principles of privacy, safety, and security. Thus, AI stakeholders need to actively promote social fairness, impartiality, and non-discrimination by taking an inclusive approach that would make the benefits of AI become pervasive \[[25](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_25)\]. Since biased data is potentially the source of sustained social injustice in AI ecosystem, urgent reforms in data curation practices, particularly based on machine learning, are the need of the hour. The risk, of course, is that without proper legal and ethical framework, decades of progress in human rights and civil liberties may be put to risk. Systems promoted by discriminatory tendencies through AI have a history of putting marginalized sections at line. It is a trend derived from bias in representation, classification, and categorization of individuals in data for ages. There is a critical need to foster ethics among technology developers. These serious implications of design flaws in AI systems, and their innate bias leading to unfair discrimination, have rebooted age-old debates about social fairness and surveillance based on racial and gender identity. Incidents have arisen in which AI algorithms differentially treat individuals on issues of race and gender, pointing at the fact that AI can potentiality amplify discriminatory practices. There is an absolute need for a thoroughgoing overhaul of the approaches to curating AI data \[[26](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_26)\].

### Intersectionality and AI: Reimagining Ethical AI

Intersectionality is a theoretically critical lens to understand the complex web of interconnectedness and interdependencies across diverse social categories and systems. It provides a mindset and lexicon that enables us to understand how the varied members of certain groups navigate their experiences in different contexts \[[27](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_27)\]. Intersectionality tries to elaborate the dimensions of ethnicity, class, sexual orientations, among many other social locations take place in such interconnections in a unique and diverse manner and thus, present the complex nature of social justice and injustice. The sensibilities developed through intersectional differences and augmented by our awareness, increases the possibility of breaking down institutional barriers and ultimately reaches full potential in doing socially transformative work. Therefore, intersectionality should be perceived as a seminal notion where knowledge becomes situated within definite environmental placements whilst reflecting the current socio-political and economic power structures \[[28](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_28)\]. Coined by Kimberle Williams Crenshaw in 1989, one of the leading proponents of critical race theory and the civil rights movements, ‘intersectionality’ delves into the complex interrelations of different social identities and their respective systems of oppression or discrimination. This framework illustrates how dynamic categories, that is, gender, race, socio-economic status, ability, sexual orientation, religion, caste, age, or nationality intersect to actually inform individual experience at any given time. The theory fosters discussion on both inter-group and intra-group differences while reflecting intersectionality of diverse oppressions in individual lives. The concept of intersectionality derives from ‘Black feminist’ thought but offers a fuller, more nuanced understanding of the manifold complexities of identity and power \[[29](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_29)\]. The theory of intersectionality brings to light better understandings of international human rights, and goes a long way in outlining how certain constructs of identity, like race and gender, combine to mould specific experiences of discrimination and persecution. This is especially important in the context of today's human rights, whereby acts of sexual violence in armed conflict are often targeted against individuals on grounds of both gender and ethnicity. In this regard, the failure to recognize human rights abuses and their intersectional dynamism, therefore, diminishes the remedies that accrue to the victims. The historical context from which the intersectionality theory evolved as a conceptual framework finds its roots in the critical race, feminism, and human rights jurisprudence from the scholarly literature review. The idea of intersectionality has been used in the broad domain of critical feminist understandings in legal, political, and sociological areas for an expression of complexity between the oppressions of multiplicity and mutual constitutions. It illuminates these identities and systems of oppression in ways that are both manifold and interrelated by providing a nuanced and contextually sensitive approach to tackling issues of social justice and inequality \[[30](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_30)\]. However, in a time when increased influence of AI over societal constructs and individual life is dominating, it becomes imperative to critically look at the power relations and biases sustained in these AI technologies. In direct contrast, the theoretical paradigm of intersectionality provides a strong lens through which we could envision AI, with the recentring of institutional power dynamics and also reflecting perspectives from queer, feminist, and critical race theories.

Crenshaw, in her theory of ‘intersectionality,’ tries to explain how various social identities and its related systems of oppression cross more than one axis simultaneously \[[29](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_29)\]. It raises a call towards deeper social realities, at the base of oppression facilitated by AI as subgroup fairness not merely a technical issue. Intersectionality rejects that there is inherent inequality in society and suggests, on the contrary, that social inequalities are systematically designed and perpetuated. Despite having a strong democratic potential, digital technologies most often come with algorithmic systems. Pre-existing biases would only be further exacerbated by digital technologies, which may be masked as cultural misinterpretation of AI as objective truth, though human intervention is involved at every stage. A case in point is COMPAS; a US court algorithm that predicts recidivism. It showed discrimination against black people since it marked them as likely future criminals far more, compared to their white counterparts \[[31](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_31)\].

Such an intersectional lens applied to AI fairness pushes us past just rolling out equity constraints on models and dig deep into what dynamics are at play in society with AI inflicted oppression and how we empower them to fix the technology. This perception shows the deep-rooted social inequities and their perpetuation through centres of influence. That power within AI still lies in human choice, system design, data collection, deployment, and interpretations of fairness that would necessarily determine who gets what at the community level with the intersection of various domains \[[32](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_32)\]. Discussion on power thus becomes indispensable at every point of the AI process. So, fairness research of AI should be important in developing just systems. An intersectional approach precludes one from restricting themselves to the first layer of making models technically fair. Rather, what is required is a very thorough analysis of the social facts behind technology driven oppression and, thereafter, help us improve the technology accordingly. This approach emphasizes the interconnections that exist between power dynamics attached to different kinds of identity and social positionalities, such as race, class, gender, among others and premises the fact that power systems are mutually reinforcing and continuously perpetuate unfair outcomes and distinct social experiences within these hierarchies. Identifying these centres of power and analyzing the way they interact gives one a way into how to dismantle inequities \[[33](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_33)\]. In the traditional AI fairness models, paradigms such as equality of opportunity, intended to neutralize biases to have equal gains across the considered sub-groups, do not guarantee substantive fairness. This is because most AI algorithms will simply reflect and propagate the systems of intersecting oppressions in the input data. They argue that realizing substantive fairness in algorithms requires actively challenging and even destabilizing the very systems of domination that are producing biased algorithms in the first place \[[34](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_34)\].

Therefore, strong fairness is AI, which requires radically different assumptions about the role of algorithms would help attain a robust mechanism. It would take the approach that questions the systemic oppression and moves towards building a more just society, rather than a reactive approach involving applying ‘debiasing’ after the fact \[[33](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_33)\]. This will mean that AI fairness transcends technical complexities; rather, it is a socio-political quagmire that demands an intersectional understanding of power dynamics and systemic injustices, including the implications that underpin algorithmic decision-making \[[35](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_35)\]. Intersectionality emerges as a potent framework for deciphering and reshaping the entrenched power dynamics inherent in AI systems. But if we apply critical, intersectional frameworks to engagement with AI systems, then maybe we can draw connections across axes of identity, and in so doing, imagine new AI paradigms that really do disrupt the patterns of marginalization and ‘othering’ that are repackaged and engineered into these technologies \[[27](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_27)\]. Intersectionality places us to be in a position to unpack and dismantle the very workings through which AI tends to reproduce power hierarchies and systems of oppression, hence setting a footing towards imagining AI systems that serve equity, inclusion, and social justice. This perspective will serve in bringing out how an intersectional approach to AI would make use of fluid identities, take stereotypes to court, and so use the instability to redefine gender roles and individual agency. And that is exactly where the importance of intersectionality comes in, to be able to negotiate critically the complex power structures and biases literally inhabiting these technologies at the time of design and deployment of AI.

## FROM BIAS TO FEMINIST APPROACH: TOWARDS AN EQUITABLE AND INCLUSIVE AI

In an era where the rapid proliferation of AI systems is taking place, critically interrogating how these technologies come to intersect with the deepening prevailing power dynamics, disparities, and structures of oppression is of paramount importance \[[36](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_36)\]. Yet, working in the space of AI ethics, rife with issues related to prejudice, partiality, and justice, has faced challenges in translating theory into practice. Within these gaps, feminist theory comes up as an illuminating framework to hold the AI sector responsible for the way the design and implementation of AI contribute to good rather than bad adjustments to support and undermine the pursuit of social justice \[[37](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_37)\]. In feminist theory, AI is used in studying, describing, and challenging present inequalities and power dynamics, bringing integration to intersectional feminist theory and AI \[[38](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_38)\]. This is quite critical, especially at a moment when digitized tools are mostly viewed as a proper panacea for societal problems, more so without paying closer attention to the inherent prejudices and predispositions within such technologies. Thinking about and applying new technologies is something that engages preferences and choices, but it also opens the chance to shape them in a way to not do harm and change their ways to maximally advance transformative, positive potentials of society.

From the feminist AI paradigm, knowledge as resulting from data-driven processes appears to be a type of mentality that is shaped by various kinds of societal perspectives, including those of gender and ethnicity, among others \[[39](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_39)\]. This consideration has particular pertinence to the ethical data management for AI when taken into realization that machine learning algorithms are by nature able to absorb and reinforce such perspectives since they have to determine whose perspectives are being reflected inside a dataset. In this regard, such a constructivist view of knowledge is well adopted by both intersectional and post-structuralist feminisms to enlighten the nexus between cognition and influence. Ethically, the responsible data handling for AI involves asking for the perspectives of all who are involved in the multi-stage process of training data preparation; structuring datasets, generating data, devising the labels and their representation, annotation, and labelling \[[40](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_40)\]. Each layer of predisposition harbours the potential to be assimilated and perpetuated by a machine learning algorithm.

The feminist epistemological approach to ethical data curation is an approach grounded on feminist theory that provides a framework to look at the theoretical underpinnings and concepts that inherently are part of AI data. Building on the feminist theoretical framework, an ethical framework is built to meet discrimination and bias in AI data. Seen from this perspective, the biases that AI perpetrates are not new but rather continuities of existing patterns of gendered and cultural disparities that are being entrenched and amplified by digital technologies. Feminist AI points out that the reason for algorithmic bias is not unconscious sexism or racism of programmers or even the training data filled with errors. Instead, it comes from the techno-culture systematically not allowing the influence and power of women and people on the margin \[[41](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_41)\]. This view tries to explain the structural mechanisms that are in general part of the structural injustice per se, which is fashioned by the social hierarchies underlying opportunities and life chances, both in terms of material and social resources, of persons within those hierarchies.

One of the biggest problems with AI bias and discrimination is the traceability of identifying who is at fault for the injustices carried out by such systems. The injustice of the structure is different from fault-based injustices, in the sense that though the latter is traced to identifiable agents or actions, there is an untraceability that characterizes structural injustice; it is very difficult to find who is to be blamed \[[18](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_18)\]. That does not, however, let the AI sector off the hook, but it throws into the limelight the urgency of the need for a very fundamental shift in how AI development and deployment issues are handled \[[42](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_42)\]. Feminist AI is the comprehensive, equal approach that operates towards the creation of a more even world. The term ‘feminist’ also goes further, in the case of gender parity, which does, of course, remain a salient aspect but extends itself to include harnessing AI for the reduction of inequalities experienced by traditionally marginalized societal groups based on race, culture, and identity intersections. Implied in this concept is the recognition that the inequities come from cultural diversities, and in so doing, recognize distinctive traditions and values characteristic of a region \[[43](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_43)\]. It is, therefore, the systemic biases that are characterized by gender, race, social status, linguistics, and other intersectionalities that tend to permeate the current artificial intelligence processes of being emanating from the Global North and subsequently replicated in the Global South. To fight and correct these prejudices and discriminatory practices through analysis and proposals informed by a feminist, decolonial, and contextualized perspective is a need \[[36](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_36)\]. They have turned into an absolute necessity to give rise to another alternative vision that answers to the challenges developed by the regions of the Global South.

It is therefore important, for this perspective of feminists to be drawn into the design and development of AI. Feminist AI provides a just, equitable and inclusive framework to finally critique and challenge a techno-culture that has been, for too long, the contributor to the exclusion of marginalized communities \[[39](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_39)\]. This technology can move down the road towards ethical data curation, anti-racist, and anti-discriminatory development of algorithms with only feminist epistemologies and intersectional approaches as sidekicks. In this light, feminist AI might indeed offer an interesting reading lens with which to look, in a critical way, at the dominating stories and practices of technology that systematically have excluded, in a systemic way, women and minoritized groups from the possibility of leadership and decision-making roles. This perspective foregrounds the structural processes and power dynamics forming the context of systemic injustice, being inherently intersectional and mediated through the interplay of social positions that regulate opportunities, resources, and life prospects for different individuals and collectives.

Integrating feminist theories, knowledge, methodologies, and epistemologies with AI might show us how such technologies could exacerbate, reproduce, or otherwise challenge current inequalities and power relations \[[38](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_38)\]. Such an approach acknowledges that the data carried is constructed from particular social perspectives, influenced by variables such as sex, ethnicity, socio-economic status, and cultural milieu. From an ethical perspective, managing data in AI requires acknowledging and admitting the parties that data may affect in terms of misrepresentation or exclusion. Utilizing a feminist epistemological approach in data curation means applying the principles of feminist theory to develop ethical frameworks which would guard against discrimination and biases in the data used for AI systems \[[40](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_40)\]. This involves thoughtful consideration of the layers of perspective involved in the data processing, including shaping the structures of datasets, generating data, defining labels, and annotating information. Any bias within such datasets runs the risk of being absorbed by machine learning algorithms and perpetuated, further damaging systemic inequalities.

Feminist AI aims at the realization of a global movement, with special pull from the Global South, offering alternative imaginations and solutions towards realization for equity, social justice, and environmental sustainability \[[39](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_39)\]. This approach unveils that AI-perpetrated injustices are not new; rather, they are an uncovered and increased intensification of pre-existing patterns of gendered, racial, and cultural inequities encoded within digital technologies. Together with critical reflection, methodological innovation, and experimentation, feminist AI is committed to contributing in ways that favour the rise of inclusive and equitable technologies that area residents face in every corner of the globe.

To this end, AI researchers should deploy an approach that places a feminist perspective to ensure that the technologies propagating existing prejudiced-based technologies and social stratification are held accountable. This means more than just increasing the representation of women in the field, although such efforts are important in their own right. It calls for a full reassessment in the way data is not just gathered and judged but also how AI is tested and used. A feminist AI approach, might also reflect on and understand how individuals from different intersecting axes of identity channelize their experience within this system of oppression. For instance, the experience of a black woman with AI will entirely be different from that of a white woman, and analysis has to be intersectional to capture this. If these built-in biases are not questioned during the creation and deployment of AI, they will further the existing inequities, especially of women and people of colour. For building AI systems that are equitable and effective, it is the social justice approach with intersectionality that becomes utmost necessary in bringing down the oppressive structures \[[27](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_27)\]. A feminist perspective is bound to help the researchers in developing and adopting AI technologies that confront head on with the systematic biases, and at the same time become inclusive with the distinct needs and experiences of all communities. Not until there is a concerted effort to infuse feminist principles at every stage of AI development, will we be able to harness the real potential of AI's transformation while guarding against the perpetuation of harmful inequalities.

## RESPONSIBLE AI

In a world where artificial intelligence technologies are penetrating fast into society at every aspect, the demand for responsible and ethical AI practices has become imperative. Responsible AI is an approach towards a human-centric style that builds users’ trust in the ethical processes of decision-making \[[44](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_44)\]. What this simply means is that the automatic determinations are fair, just, impartial, based on good-will intentions, not discriminative, and in conformance with societal regulations and norms. Transparent and explainable AI systems that securely operationalize its use make sure that the objective of responsible AI, met with the aim of allowing users to understand decisions made by AI systems while protecting the privacy of the user \[[45](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_45)\]. Of high importance is the issue of trust for any AI application to be accepted and taken up in society; therefore, every stage of its development and deployment has to follow through with trust as one of its key objectives. It remains one of the serious challenges; nevertheless, how salient this may be-trustworthy AI has not been fully appreciated. One of the principal lines along which AI ethical issues cut is that of fairness and the tendency to perpetuate vicious biases.

### Ethical AI and Fairness

Thus, transparent and accountable AI inherently links the two to the needs of justice, fairness, and equity; that is, just, fair, and equitable distribution of power, access, opportunities, and outcomes. The problem of AI explainability is thus quite a paramount ethical issue \[[46](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_46)\]. In this direction, the right to explanation enshrined as part of the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) goes in the direction to make AI more ethically acceptable, which involves direct impacts on people from decisions by the AI system. Most AI systems rely on historical data and, in the process, unconsciously pave the way for biases \[[47](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_47)\]. This points at the need for techniques that can detect and mitigate bias to be incorporated at the development life cycle of AI \[[48](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_48)\]. These strategies identify and mitigate biases systematically, either in data or algorithms, so that they do not propagate into discriminatory outcomes by the AI systems. Not only do such methods advance equity and ethical decision-making, but they also assist in conformity to the regulations that require impartial outcomes in any decision-making process and therefore promote trust amongst the interested parties.

### Privacy and Security in AI

The assurance with AI relies mostly on the privacy and security of the methodologies used. Ensuring such privacy and secure AI methodologies could reduce the risks attached, which are the cause of private data exposure and privacy infringements leading to unauthorized use. Data privacy is responsible for proper handling, processing, storage, and usage of personal information that maintains and ensures all the rights of individuals in relation to their personal data \[[49](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_49)\]. This, therefore, makes data security a prerequisite to the same. More focus should be given to ensuring the privacy resilience of AI systems increasingly by guaranteeing the safety and preservation of persons’ privacy while allowing the collection and use of data. This may include obtaining user consent prior to any practices of data collection or usage, to ensure the protection of sensitive user information from unauthorized access and misuse, including data anonymization and minimization, and ensuring that privacy risk management is woven into each part of AI development and deployment \[[50](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_50)\].

### Interpretability and Explainability

Obfuscation, the former quintessential characteristic of the AI sector, is a very traditional way for firms to safeguard their trade secrets not only from the highly competitive market but also from the inner machinations of their algorithms. However, it is above and beyond the issue of respect for intellectual property; there is a new requirement arising with respect to AI. That is, systems of ‘explicability’ and ‘interpretability’ of AI, especially those based on deep learning, since most of them are already incomprehensible in explaining intelligible mechanisms to humans \[[46](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_46)\]. And this is what people refer to as the ‘black box’ of neural networks, wherein data inputs are understood, of course, and outputs are understood, but not the algorithmic determinations themselves. We are increasingly deferring to algorithmic decisions without an understanding of the reasoning behind them. We are therefore eroding our capacities of thinking and judgment over our own norms and assumptions. In that regard, there remains a huge incentive for us to rely on algorithms since they have emerged to be core aspects of human welfare. However, the ethical approach, oriented towards human well-being, demands transparency and interpretability \[[50](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_50)\].

The establishment of trust and confidence in the outputs from AI is central to the explainable and interpretable AI approach, which advocates for transparency and, most importantly, lucidity in the AI operations \[[44](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_44)\]. The latter refers to the preference that AI systems be transparent and interpretable to the extent that the decision processes are understandable for the users, consumers, developers, watchdog groups, and other involved external parties. “Developing AI systems that do not only make clear determinations but also offer intelligible elucidations can aid users to comprehend the rationale underpinning AI-based decisions.”

Anthropocentric design practices also employ explicability techniques: feature significance analysis, model visualization, and rule-based models, allowing increased explicability and interpretability. In such a context, it may also serve as the right way to balance transparency with ‘need to know’ access and allow trust to be bred, assure compliance with evolving privacy regulations, and get the most quality out of the outcomes.

### Governance and Social Good

The perils and prospects of AI have culminated in an ecosystem replete with a multitude of initiatives that endeavour to provide guidance on how AI should be developed, adopted, and utilized to advance the ‘AI for Social Good’ agenda. “These initiatives can be categorized into three broad categories:

1.  **AI and Ethics:** Ethical frameworks and guidelines promoting and fostering responsible AI.
    
2.  **AI and Governance:** Governance mechanisms operationalizing responsible AI, including auditing mechanisms, risk assessments, standards, certifications, corporate governance frameworks, and more.
    
3.  **AI and Social Good:** Applied projects advancing the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) responsibly.” \[[16](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_16)\]
    

Such efforts are usually indicative of the urgency that AI must be of social benefit, with the benefits of AI ‘markedly’ outweighing its risks and the ways in which misuses of this technology are forecasted. They support AI systems that are both designed to engender and reproduce inequitable biases, are safe and tested, are responsible to human beings, and designed per the privacy design principles. They also need to meet high scientific excellence standards and their availability for use should be according to the ethical tenets. The needs for socially beneficial AI applications are immense and provoke various quandaries, especially the contribution to attaining the SDGs by 2030 \[[51](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_51)\]. AI technologies, on the other hand, have reached a very mature stage and more and more infiltrate an immense diversity of applications across the industrial and economic sectors, cultural and artistic domains. Responsible development and deployment of AI become essential in drawing its potential for solving world challenges with the least possible detriments.

Responsible AI deals with many dimensions; it is more of a multipronged and holistic practice in ethical decision-making, fairness, privacy, and security, interpretability and explainability, governance, and social good \[[44](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_44)\]. Prior to that, for the prioritization of ensuring trust, transparency, and accountability across the entire AI development life cycle, it is recommended that due consideration be given to ways that help ensure stakeholder confidence and provide reasonable assurance that AI systems contribute positively to their respective organizational objectives and to the general societal well-being. This includes extensive risk assessments and risk mitigation strategies that focus on AI algorithm transparency, embedding transparency and accountability in the AI practices, and putting strong controls in place for privacy \[[45](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_45)\]. Finally, the integration of bias detection and mitigation techniques is of paramount importance to ensure that the perpetration of such discrimination is obviated for AI systems to operate fairly. In this still-developing landscape, therefore, it calls for precise legal and regulatory frameworks defining the role and duties of “trustworthy AI” and “responsible AI” \[[49](#chapter17.xhtml#r17_49)\]. These should be well spelled out in the realization of not only the trust but also the responsible AI, with ethical and transparent decision-making that advances accountability and the good for the society. Embracing this approach would guarantee that, on the one hand, societies could benefit to the maximum from the revolutionary impacts that such technologies harbour, and on the other hand, save them from attendant risks that could bring serious injury to the principles of social justice, equity, and human well-being.

## CONCLUSION

Prejudice and discriminatory practices have constantly been a consistent quandary in the development and deployment of artificial intelligence technologies. Making it worse is the fact that biases in AI systems do, in fact, represent a real threat to equality, inclusiveness, and social justice-from furthering negative stereotypes to reinforcing those that exist. This challenge goes beyond the call of ethical imperatives. One can easily say that the very concept of intersectionality is the best way to understand the many dimensions of oppression and marginalization. Such a framework is based on the understanding that there are several social identities, operating at many levels, most often intersecting and simultaneously shaping human experiences within given systems of power. So, within the context of AI, it is at this juncture that intersectionality comes in to help us understand how such multiple interacting identities lead to systems and structures of discrimination that may be different or compounded and that undergird the pressing urgency of inclusive and culturally responsive technologies. Feminist AI is built on the tenets of intersectionality to provide a critical framework which begins to answer the systemic biases and inequalities that have marked the history of technological innovation. Each of these moves in Feminist AI questions the dominant techno-culture, which has always put away women and people with marginalization from the table of leadership and decision-making by the participation of feminist epistemologies and situated knowledges. This requires, above all, a paradigm shift in the collection of data, design of algorithms, and testing and deployment of AI systems where the voices of those historically at the margins are centred. It identifies that reaching substantive justice in AI will indeed require more than mere ‘debiasing’ of algorithms; it has to be a proactive way that is lively in actively challenging oppression and making society fairer across various cultural contexts. This intersectional and feminist approach commensurate with the principles of Responsible AI, which is an ethical paradigm of the decision-making process in the development of AI, focusing on fairness and social good. This fact underlines the importance of risk assessment, a mitigating strategy, transparency in AI algorithms, and measures for accountability to ensure that AI systems contribute positively towards the achievement of organizational objectives and add to the well-being of society. The guiding lights of Feminist AI and intersectionality, integrated within the framing of Responsible AI, might give a glimmer of hope towards this, otherwise densely complex area of research, where identity and its intersections with power and oppression hold sway.

Ultimately, the ethical development of AI is responsible by not only a moral duty, but it is a prerequisite step towards Sustainable Development Goals. AI has the potential to be potent tool in aiding positive changes, innovations, and leading progress in healthcare, education, environmental protection, and economic development. However, to make full use of this potential, the need of the hour is to evolve an integrated and holistic approach that takes into dimension these dimensions, feminism, and responsible practices at all stages of AI development and deployment life cycle. A reflective, transparent, and accountable ethical environment fosters AI practices first to the fore of social justice, equality, and the well-being of humankind and, thereby, benefits humanity and the planet together. This opens the window to truly unlock the potential of AI and potential that may facilitate more than mere transformation: it may indeed facilitate power that will uplift and celebrate the very rich diversity of human experiences and cultural expressions.
